Monodromy Bootstrap for 𝖲𝖴 ⁡ ( 2 | 2 ) \mathsf{SU}(2|2) Quantum Spectral Curves: From Hubbard model to AdS 3 /CFT 2
Simon Ekhammar a, Dmytro Volin a,b

Abstract
We propose a procedure to derive quantum spectral curves of AdS/CFT type by requiring that a specially designed analytic continuation around the branch point results in an automorphism of the underlying algebraic structure. In this way we derive four new curves. Two are based on 𝖲𝖴 ⁡ ( 2 | 2 ) \mathsf{SU}(2|2) symmetry, and we show that one of them, under the assumption of square root branch points, describes Hubbard model. Two more are based on 𝖲𝖴 ⁡ ( 2 | 2 ) × 𝖲𝖴 ⁡ ( 2 | 2 ) \mathsf{SU}(2|2)\times\mathsf{SU}(2|2) . In the special subcase of zero central charge, they both reduce to the unique nontrivial curve which furthermore has analytic properties compatible with 𝖯𝖲𝖴 ⁡ ( 1 , 1 | 2 ) × 𝖯𝖲𝖴 ⁡ ( 1 , 1 | 2 ) \mathsf{PSU}(1,1|2)\times\mathsf{PSU}(1,1|2) real form. A natural conjecture follows that this is the quantum spectral curve of AdS/CFT integrable system with AdS 3 × {}_{3}\times S 3 × {}^{3}\times T 4 background supported by RR-flux. We support the conjecture by verifying its consistency with the massive sector of asymptotic Bethe equations in the large volume regime. For this spectral curve, it is compulsory that branch points are not of the square root type which qualitatively distinguishes it from the previously known cases.



UUITP-44/21

NORDITA 2021-090

1
Introduction and main results

Study of AdS
5
/CFT
4
integrability revealed a surprisingly simple set of Riemann-Hilbert equations – quantum spectral curve (QSC)
Gromov:2013pga
;
Gromov:2014caa
. While, on one hand, it is related to the system of Q-functions known for a large class of integrable models, its analytic structure, on the other hand, is rather peculiar and unusual. Unlike in most other cases, Q-functions of AdS/CFT integrability are non-meromorphic functions of the spectral parameter
u
. They have branch points, in fact infinitely many of them, at positions determined by the ’t Hooft coupling constant. The Riemann-Hilbert equations define the monodromy around the branch points and we witness a non-trivial interplay between the analytic and the algebraic properties of the system.

Among many questions to address, one is how unique AdS/CFT integrability is. The spectral curve for AdS
5
/CFT
4
seems to exploit a lot the properties of the underlying
\mathsf{PSU}(2,2|4)
symmetry. Can we get a similar construction for other (super-)groups? So far the only other example is AdS
{}_{4}/
CFT
3
QSC
Cavaglia:2014exa
;
Bombardelli:2017vhk
based on
\mathsf{OSp}(6|4)

1
1

1

Riemann-Hilbert equations for the latter are very similar to those of AdS
5
/CFT
4
, it is even tempting to ask whether it is as a genuinely distinct QSC. There are also ways to formally reduce the symmetry by introducing boundary effects

Gromov:2015dfa
;
Kazakov:2015efa
or to deform the symmetry
Klabbers:2017vtw
. We do not consider the resulting QSC’s as novel because Riemann-Hilbert equations remain the same.
. It is as well unclear whether we can build different QSC’s with the same underlying symmetry. In this paper we propose a systematic approach to address these questions and exhibit it for the derivation of spectral curves with
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
and
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)\times\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
symmetries
2
2

2

We follow an imprecise physical jargon and label as
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
any
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{2|2}
Q-systems. As we shall see, concrete real forms are not always compact, they emerge naturally dictated by the monodromy features; and unimodularity appears only through asymptotic behaviour imposed on Q-functions. Notation
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
in the sense of the compact real form appears only in relation to Hubbard model discussed in Section
6
.
. We choose
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
because it seems to be the simplest example where one can get non-trivial features. Higher-rank cases is a subject for future research.

The following strategy which further develops ideas of Section 3 in
Gromov:2014caa
is proposed: First, we introduce the
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
Q-system as a purely algebro-geometric object with QQ-relations understood as fused Plücker relations. This part of the construction is universal and does not depend on particular analytic properties of Q’s. In the next step, we introduce basic analytic properties, mainly the presence of certain branch cuts and certain domains of analyticity is postulated so as to get an analogy with the AdS
5
/CFT
4
case and insure that the system does not get too complicated. Finally, the cornerstone follows which we call the monodromy bootstrap: informally, the analytic continuation around the branch point should result in an automorphism of the Q-system.

While having an automorphism generated by a non-trivial monodromy is a rather standard idea, here we are dealing with
non-local
QQ-relations in which case defining of a consistent analytic continuation is far from obvious: the same function entering a non-local equation is evaluated at different values of the spectral parameter which can then encircle different branch points upon the continuation, they are even forced to do so in the case of AdS/CFT integrability. An example is shown in Figure to the right where the two different values are marked with crosses. The monodromy bootstrap is equipped with a procedure that overcomes this difficulty.

In the outcome, the monodromy bootstrap requirement turns out to be very restrictive and we end up with only as many systems as the number of ‘outer’ automorphism classes. For Q-systems based on
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
symmetry we get two results, of type A and B (no physical meaning behind the labelling). The type-A corresponds to the trivial automorphism class, whereas the type-B corresponds to Hodge duality (essentially the transposition of the Dynkin diagram).

Assuming square root cuts, the type-B system becomes a quantum spectral curve that, as we show, describes Hubbard model. Hubbard model plays an important role in condensed matter applications and was intensively studied
HubbardBook
. QSC as a consistent analytic Q-system was not described for it so far although
Cavaglia:2015nta
contains an equivalent set of equations.

For Q-systems based on
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)\times\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
symmetry, we insist on a nontrivial interplay between the left and the right group. Then two QSC’s can be built, we label them C,D. Assuming zero central charges, cases C and D become equivalent. Furthermore, the non-compact real form
\mathsf{PSU}(1,1|2)\times\mathsf{PSU}(1,1|2)
turns out to be the one consistent with the monodromy properties. Hence we get the unique QSC with the AdS/CFT-type cut structure and the stated symmetry. Given its uniqueness, it is natural to conjecture that it is the quantum spectral curve for AdS
3
/CFT
2
integrability with AdS
{}_{3}\times
S
{}^{3}\times
T
4
background supported by RR-flux, probably the simplest case in the class of integrable AdS
3
/CFT
2
models
Zarembo:2010sg
;
Zarembo:2010yz
. We make first checks towards confirming the conjecture and investigate the massive sector in the large volume regime comparing it with asymptotic Bethe equations.

An important distinction of the derived low-rank QSC’s compared to the ones for AdS
5
/CFT
4
and AdS
4
/CFT
3
is that they almost never admit Q-functions with square root branch points, the only exception is Hubbard model. We offer no-go theorems and their systematic proofs. They guarantee that no opportunities for systems with square root cuts were missed for cases A,B,C, of course assuming a set of axioms about Q-system that we propose to use.

The paper is organised as follows: After recalling the algebraic relations of
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
Q-system in Section
2
, we postulate and motivate the basic axioms about analytic properties of Q-functions (Properties 1-4) and give a precise definition of the monodromy bootstrap in Section
3
, in particular we explain how to overcome the controversy between the non-locality of the equations and the need to unambiguously define the analytic continuation. Section
4
derives, on the example of the type-B system, Riemann-Hilbert equations to be obeyed by Q-functions as a consequence of the monodromy bootstrap. Section
5
offers these equations for all four cases A-D presented in the form of
{\bf P}\mu
-systems analogous to the one in
Gromov:2013pga
. The derivation of case A is given in Appendix
A
, we don’t give details about derivations for cases C and D because they require only cosmetic adjustments obvious from the stated in the paper equations. Section
6
is dedicated to the specialisation of the type-B model to the case of square root branch points, we show that the resulting QSC can encode, depending on the choice of the source term, both the spectrum of (inhomogeneous) Hubbard model and the corresponding thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz equations. Section
7
considers case C (or, equivalently, D) under assumption of zero central charge and demonstrates that the resulting QSC is compatible, in the large volume regime, with asymptotic Bethe equations of the AdS
3
/CFT
2
integrable system, at least in the massive sector. Section
8
is devoted to conclusions, discussion, and outlook. Appendix
B
gives proofs of no-go theorems.

2
Algebraic structure of Q-system

In this section we review the algebraic structure of
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
Q-system using notations tailored to this specific low-rank case. For an in-depth discussion of general
\mathfrak{gl}_{\mathsf{m}|\mathsf{n}}
systems see
e.g.
Tsuboi:2009ud
;
Tsuboi:2011iz
;
Kazakov:2015efa
.

2.1
Geometric construction

Consider a function of the spectral parameter
u
and four Grassmann variables
\theta^{1},\theta^{2},\theta^{3},\theta^{4}
:

\displaystyle B(u;{\bf\theta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B_{\emptyset}(u+\mathtt{i})+B_{m}(u+\mathtt{i}/2)\theta^{m}+\frac{1}{2}B_{mn}(u)\theta^{m}\theta^{n}

(1)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}B_{mnr}(u-\mathtt{i}/2)\theta^{m}\theta^{n}\theta^{r}+B_{1234}(u-\mathtt{i})\theta^{1}\theta^{2}\theta^{3}\theta^{4}\,.

This function should satisfy the following relation

\displaystyle B(u;\theta)=B_{\emptyset}^{++}+B_{(1)}^{+}\frac{B(u-\mathtt{i};\theta)}{B_{\emptyset}}\,,

(2)

where we used the standard notation for the shift of spectral parameter:
f^{+}(u):=f(u+\mathtt{i}/2)
,
f^{[n]}(u):=f(u+\mathtt{i}\,n/2)
.

Relation (
2
) determines, by recursion, all the homogeneous components of
B
through
B_{\emptyset}
and
B_{(1)}\equiv B_{m}\theta^{m}
, and there is a clear geometric interpretation: Think about
B_{(1)}
as a 1-form that defines a line in
\mathbb{C}^{4}
. The recursion implies
B_{(2)}=\frac{B_{(1)}^{+}B_{(1)}^{-}}{B_{\emptyset}}
, hence
B_{(2)}
defines a 2-dimensional plane spanned by
B_{(1)}^{+}
and
B_{(1)}^{-}
; and
B_{(3)}=\frac{B_{(1)}^{[2]}B_{(1)}B_{(1)}^{[-2]}}{B_{\emptyset}^{+}B_{\emptyset}^{-}}
, hence
B_{(3)}
defines a 3-dimensional plane. So, the homogeneous components of
B
are Plücker coordinates of Grassmanians of
\mathbb{C}^{4}
and (
2
) summarises fused Plücker relations.

Now, perform an odd Fourier transform with respect to variables
\theta^{3},\theta^{4}
:

\displaystyle Q(u;\theta^{1},\theta^{2},\psi^{1},\psi^{2})=\int d\theta^{3}\,d\theta^{4}\,e^{\theta^{3}\psi^{1}+\theta^{4}\psi^{2}}B(u,\theta)\,.

(3)

Q
is explicitly parameterised as:

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q_{\emptyset|\emptyset}(u)+Q_{a|\emptyset}(u-i/2)\theta^{a}+Q_{\emptyset|i}(u+i/2)\psi^{i}+Q_{a|i}(u)\theta^{a}\psi^{i}+\ldots+Q_{12|12}(u)\,\theta^{1}\theta^{2}\psi^{1}\psi^{2}\,.

(4)

In this way we got what can be called the
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{2|2}
Q-system: a collection of
2^{2+2}=16
Q-functions of the spectral parameter
u
that satisfy (
2
) also known as the QQ-relations in this context.

Indices
a,b,\ldots
will be called bosonic and indices
i,j,\ldots
will be called fermionic. This naming is a purely terminological convention. Independently of the index structure, all Q-functions are
\mathbb{C}
-valued functions of the spectral parameter.

2.2
Symmetries

The symmetries of the Q-system, that is transformations that preserve the QQ-relations, have a transparent meaning in the above-described geometric set up. There are three types of them:

Hodge duality.

Instead of considering hyper-planes, one could consider their Hodge duals. For instance, instead of parameterising a line by
B_{(1)}
, one could parameterise a 3-dimensional plane in the dual space etc.

To reflect this duality in formulas, it is handy to introduce the Hodge-dual

\displaystyle Q^{A|I}\equiv(-1)^{|B||I|}\epsilon^{AB}\epsilon^{IJ}Q_{B|J}\,,

(5)

with the inverse Hodge-dual given by

\displaystyle Q_{A|I}=(-1)^{|B||I|}\epsilon_{BA}\epsilon_{JI}Q^{B|J}\,,

(6)

where
\epsilon
is the Levi-Civita symbol with the normalisation
\epsilon_{12}=\epsilon^{12}=1
, and
A,B,I,J
are multi-indices from the set
\{1,2\}
.

Hodge-duality is explicitly the following transformation

\displaystyle Q_{A|I}\mapsto Q_{A|I}^{*}\equiv Q^{A|I}\,.

(7)

All QQ-relations remain valid under this transformation. Note also that it is not exactly an involution

\displaystyle Q_{A|I}^{**}=(-1)^{|A|+|I|}Q_{A|I}\,.

(8)

Hodge duality should be thought as a large (discrete) transformation. In bosonic Q-systems, where Q-functions are arranged in representations of
\mathfrak{sl}_{\mathsf{m}}
, Hodge duality can be viewed as the pullback under the outer automorphism originating from the reflection of the Dynkin diagram, see
e.g.
Ekhammar:2020enr
. Multiplication by a phase in (
8
) is an example of a continuous transformation, analog of an inner automorphism. The below-introduced H-rotations and gauge transformations, in contrast to Hodge duality, are symmetries of the continuous type.

It appears handy to use Q-functions and their Hodge-duals simultaneously and in particular benefit from rewriting certain QQ-relations in a form containing both upper- and lower-indices. We visually organise all interesting Q-functions in the following (Hasse) diagram
3
3

3

The only two functions which are not depicted are
Q_{12|\emptyset}=Q^{\emptyset|12}
and
Q_{\emptyset|12}=Q^{12|\emptyset}
. They can be found from the QQ-relation
Q_{12|\emptyset}Q_{\emptyset|\emptyset}=Q_{1|\emptyset}^{+}Q_{2|\emptyset}^{-}-Q_{1|\emptyset}^{-}Q_{2|\emptyset}^{+}
and the equivalent one for
Q_{\emptyset|12}
. We do not use these functions in our study.

(9)

where short-hand
Q^{b|j}
stands for
\epsilon_{ab}\epsilon_{ij}Q^{b|j}
, and similar for
Q^{\emptyset|j},Q^{b|\emptyset}
.

We summarise the explicit QQ-relations for the Q-functions from the diagram in Table
1
. All the QQ-relations follow from the above-described geometric principle, straightforwardly or after short algebraic manipulations.

Hasse diagram for the Hodge-dual system is given by

(10)

notice the difference in signs compared to (
9
).

(11)

and

Q_{a|\emptyset}=+\left(\frac{Q_{a|i}}{Q^{\emptyset|\emptyset}}\right)^{\pm}Q^{\emptyset|i}

\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@begin(14c)\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@end

Q_{\emptyset|i}=+\left(\frac{Q_{a|i}}{Q^{\emptyset|\emptyset}}\right)^{\pm}Q^{a|\emptyset}

\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@begin(14d)\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@end

B_{(1)}^{\vphantom{\pm}}B_{(2)}^{\pm}=0

Table 1:
Explicit QQ-relations. Here
e.g.
B_{(1)}B_{(2)}^{+}=0
means that the line defined by
B_{(1)}
belongs to the plane defined by
B_{(2)}^{+}
etc;
B^{*}
denotes a Hodge-dual object.

H-rotations.

Clearly, expansion (
1
) is written in a certain reference frame of
\mathbb{C}^{4}
. However, the whole construction of
B
’s is covariant, hence the Plücker relations do not change if we perform an arbitrary
{\mathsf{GL}}(4)
‘rotation’
B_{m}\to H_{m}{}^{n}B_{n}
. The elements of a
4\times 4
matrix
H
can be functions of the spectral parameter and they should be
i
-periodic,
H^{+}=H^{-}
, to comply with (
2
).

Fourier transform (
3
) partially breaks covariance, and the remaining freedom of rotations is
{\mathsf{GL}}(2)\times{\mathsf{GL}}(2)
, defined by

\displaystyle Q_{\emptyset|\emptyset}

\displaystyle\to Q_{\emptyset|\emptyset}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{a|\emptyset}

\displaystyle\to(H_{b})_{a}{}^{b}\,Q_{b|\emptyset}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{\emptyset|i}\,

\displaystyle\to(H_{f})_{i}{}^{j}\,Q_{\emptyset|j}\,,

(15)

and then extended in the obvious way to the other Q-functions, for instance

\displaystyle Q_{12|12}\to\det H_{b}^{+}\,\det H_{f}^{+}\,Q_{12|12}\,.

(16)

A reference frame chosen by means of H-rotations will be called a Q-basis.

Gauge transformations.

The overall functional rescalings

\displaystyle B_{\emptyset}\to g_{1}(u)B_{\emptyset}\,,\ \ \ {\rm and}\ \ \ B_{(1)}\to g_{2}(u)B_{(1)}

(17)

induce the corresponding rescalings of all the B-functions by demanding invariance of (
2
) and are referred to as gauge transformations
4
4

4

not to confuse with gauging of H-symmetry which can be used to relate Q-systems and finite-difference opers
Kazakov:2015efa
;
Koroteev:2018jht
;
Ekhammar:2020enr
and which we won’t use in this paper.
. All the geometric objects,
i.e.
lines and planes, are not sensible to the choice of a gauge.

We write down transformation (
17
) on the level of Q-functions. Each Q-function on Hasse diagram is being multiplied by the corresponding combination of
g_{b}(u)
and
g_{f}(u)
shown in the figure below:

(18)

where
g_{b}
,
g_{f}
have the definite functional relation to
g_{1},g_{2}
:
g_{2}=g_{f}^{+}g_{f}^{-}
and
g_{1}=\frac{g_{2}^{+}g_{2}^{-}}{g_{b}\,g_{f}}
.

A special interesting case of (
18
) is the transformation which leaves
Q_{\emptyset|\emptyset}
invariant:
g_{b}=g_{f}^{-1}
with
h\equiv g_{b}^{+}g_{b}^{-}
:

(19)

Note that rotations are slightly mixed with gauge transformations: the case
(H_{b})_{a}{}^{b}=h\,\delta_{a}{}^{b}
,
(H_{f})_{i}{}^{j}=h^{-1}\delta_{i}{}^{j}
is equivalent to (
19
) with
i
-periodic function
h
. Hence, rotations/gauge=
S({\mathsf{GL}}(2)\times{\mathsf{GL}}(2))
.

3
Monodromy bootstrap

3.1
Conventions on analytic continuation

Branch points are essential for this work, most of the functions will be multi-valued functions of the spectral parameter, typically with infinitely many Riemann sheets. This subsection is devoted to specifying conventions to address this framework.

In practice, there are functions (such as
{\bf P},{\bf Q},h,\mu,\omega
with some indices and other decorations attached to them) that have branch points at
u=\pm{\mbox{h}}+\mathtt{i}\mathbb{Z}
at least on some of the Riemann sheets; and there are functions (such as
\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{\downarrow},g,g_{\downarrow}
) that have branch points at
u=\pm{\mbox{h}}+\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2}+\mathtt{i}\mathbb{Z}
.
h
is a real positive number
5
5

5

not to confuse with gauge transformation functions of the spectral parameter
h,{\hat{\mathsf{h}}},{\check{\mathsf{h}}}
.
that depends on parameters defining the physical theory. For instance, in AdS
5
/CFT
4
QSC, it is related to the ’t Hooft coupling constant of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM as
{\mbox{h}}=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{2\pi}

Beisert:2004hm

6
6

6

For this model, the standard notation is not
h
but
g
, however the normalisation of
g
differs across the literature: it can be that
2\,{\mbox{g}}=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{2\pi}
, or
\sqrt{2}\,{\mbox{g}}=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{2\pi}
, or
{\mbox{g}}=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{2\pi}
.
. Another example that we shall present is QSC for Hubbard model where
{\mbox{h}}=\frac{1}{2\,\mathbf{u}}
with
\mathbf{u}
being the coupling constant of Hamiltonian
(2.22)
in
HubbardBook
, see Section
6
.

We say that we are in the
physical kinematics
if we define a Riemann sheet by connecting two branch points
\pm{\mbox{h}}+\mathtt{i}\,c
,
c\in\mathbb{R}
, by short cuts
[-{\mbox{h}}+\mathtt{i}\,c,{\mbox{h}}+\mathtt{i}\,c]
; and we are in the
mirror kinematics

7
7

7

The naming originates from the development of the mirror model of AdS/CFT integrability
Arutyunov:2007tc
if we connect these branch points by long cuts
(-\infty+\mathtt{i}\,c,-{\mbox{h}}+\mathtt{i}\,c]\cup[{\mbox{h}}+\mathtt{i}\,c,+\infty+\mathtt{i}\,c)
.

To write equations unambiguously, we should introduce simply-connected domains of spectral parameter
u
for each of the functions in the mirror/physical kinematics, and the gluing rule between different domains. Most functions have a bonus property: they are either UHPA or LHPA. UHPA stands for upper half-plane analytic
8
8

8

In this work ‘analytic’ and ‘meromorphic’ are used in a loose sense and really mean that a function does not have branch points at
\pm{\mbox{h}}+\ldots
. The functions might actually have other singularities whose control/cancellation would depend on a physical model. The presence of other singularities should not play role in the analysis as we assume that they do not affect the discussed monodromy properties.
meaning there are no branch points for sufficiently large
\Im(u)
on a certain Riemann sheet. LHPA stands for lower half-plane analytic with the equivalent meaning.

We glue mirror and physical kinematics in the upper half-plane for UHPA functions and in the lower half-plane for LHPA functions
9
9

9

If function is both UHPA and LHPA, we assign it by default to one of the classes depending on what is convenient. This assignment is purely technical and is not of physical significance
. If functions are neither UHPA or LHPA, which is the case for
\mu,\omega
, we glue the two kinematics through the strip
0<\Im(u)<1
.

In the table below we outline the simply-connected domains we agree to pick for functions encountered in the paper, with crosses denoting the values of
u
(and their vicinities) where the functions attain the same value both in the physical and the mirror kinematics (
i.e.
where we glue the two kinematics). We shall call them definition domains and, unless otherwise is specified, the definition domain means the one from the
physical kinematics
.

UHPA, physical

UHPA, mirror

Q

Q_{a|i},g

{\bf P},F,{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}

P

Q_{a|i},g

{\bf Q},F,{\check{\mathsf{h}}}

LHPA, mirror

{\bf Q}_{\downarrow}

\mathcal{Q}_{a}|^{i},g_{\downarrow}

{\bf P}_{\downarrow}

\mathcal{Q}_{a}|^{i},g_{\downarrow}

{\bf Q}_{\downarrow}

not-HPA physical

\mu,\omega

\mu,\omega

Large-
u
asymptotics introduced
e.g.
in (
7
) is written for the definition domain in the physical kinematics and
\Re(u)>0
. We do not assume any type of Stokes phenomena at infinity in this paper, though if a ladder of branch points is going to infinity, we of course may get different asymptotics from the right or the left of the ladder. This effect was indeed observed for
\omega_{ij}
of AdS
5
/CFT
4
QSC
Gromov:2014caa
, we suggested it by the extra vertical cut lines in the physical kinematics in figures above.

Equations in the paper are written to be valid on the intersection of the defining domains of functions entering the equations, and by default we pick the
physical kinematics
. When the mirror kinematics is used, we say it explicitly.

The notation
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{f}\vphantom{f}(u)
is always well-defined for the strip
0<\Im(u)<1
and it means, to choose
f
in the definition domain of the physical kinematics restricted to this strip and then analytically continue it
clock-wise
around branch point
h
(encircling only this branch point and no other). We assume that the analytic continuation around the cut
[-{\mbox{h}},{\mbox{h}}]
has no effect on the monodromy and so continuation around the branch point
-{\mbox{h}}
will be never considered
10
10

10

presence of the second branch point is mostly cosmetic for what concerns the monodromy properties discussed in the paper. The reason to keep it is that we know it is present in the concrete physical systems where it assures in particular the possibility to have correct large-
u
behaviour.
. The
counterclock-wise
continuation around
h
shall be denoted by
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{f}\vphantom{f}
. It is not necessarily true that
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{f}\vphantom{f}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{f}\vphantom{f}
.

If an equation involves
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{f}\vphantom{f}
or
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{f}\vphantom{f}
then it is valid as stated at least in the strip
0<\Im(u)<1
and when we use the definition domain in the physical kinematics for all involved functions.

A limited number of functions require only two sheets to be defined (the main example is function
F
for monodromy bootstrap involving Hodge duality, more emerge in asymptotic limits). To describe them it is at time practical to use Zhukovsky variable
x
related to the spectral parameter by

\displaystyle x+\frac{1}{x}=\frac{2u}{{\mbox{h}}}\,.

(20)

Two-sheeted functions of
u
with branch points at
\pm{\mbox{h}}
become meromorphic functions of
x
. For these functions, the defining domain in
u
-variable and the default choice of the branch for
x
is
|x|>1
.

3.2
Half-plane analyticity

In Section
2
we gave a purely algebraic description of the Q-system which is essentially model-independent. Now we shall be constraining the analytic structure of Q-functions by requiring step by step several properties. We shall formulate them and also provide motivation for their significance.

Property 1:

It is possible to choose a gauge and a Q-basis in which all Q-functions are meromorphic in the complex plane for sufficiently large positive value of
\Im(u)
, that is that they belong to the UHPA class. We further restrict the gauge by adjusting
Q_{\emptyset|\emptyset}=1
.

To understand the importance of this property observe that some of the QQ-relations are non-local, more precisely they contain the same function evaluated at two different values of the spectral parameter. If such a function has branch points, the non-local equation is generically ill-defined as there is no unique path connecting the two different values. One should either give a preference to one path over another (choose a kinematics) or assure that there is a domain free from branch points and then define QQ-relations in this domain. Thanks to Property 1, such domain exists in an appropriate gauge.

Property 2 makes the domain of analyticity more precise:

Property 2:

By an appropriate usage of (
19
) one can achieve, without spoiling analyticity in the upper half-plane, that
Q_{a|\emptyset}
is meromorphic outside the short cut
[-{\mbox{h}},{\mbox{h}}]
and
Q_{\emptyset|i}
is meromorphic outside the long cut with branch points at
\pm{\mbox{h}}
, see the diagrams for
{\bf P}
and
{\bf Q}
on page
3.1
.

For the above-introduced analyticity of
Q_{a|\emptyset},Q_{\emptyset|i}
, QQ-relations directly imply that
Q_{a|i}
and
Q^{\emptyset|\emptyset}
are meromorphic for
\Im(u)>-1/2
, and
Q^{a|\emptyset},Q^{\emptyset|i}
are meromorphic for
\Im(u)>0
. Now the gauge freedom is constrained: From
Q_{\emptyset|\emptyset}=1
we see that only transformations (
19
) are allowed, and, furthermore, analytic properties of
Q_{a|\emptyset},Q_{\emptyset|i}
restrict the allowed analytic properties of
h
. We denote the Q-functions in such a basis and gauge by a calligraphic font
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}
. The following short-hand notation will be also used:
{\bf P}_{a}\equiv\mathcal{Q}_{a|\emptyset}
,
{\bf Q}_{i}\equiv\mathcal{Q}_{\emptyset|i}
.

Property 2 may seem artificial. This is partially true, however there is a reason for choosing such an approach. In most of the works on quantum integrability, the dependence on a spectral parameter is considered to be uniform
11
11

11

We leave aside the cut structure emerging in the quasi-classical limit. These cuts are dynamic, they appear from condensation of Bethe roots, whereas we are discussing kinematic branch points which remain present on the quantum level.
. The notable exception is AdS/CFT integrability where an infinite tower of Zhukovsky branch points is introduced and the functions of the spectral parameter are no longer uniform. This phenomenon requires better study; one of the aims of this work is to show how to reconcile the non-locality of the functional equations,
e.g.
QQ-relations, and the presence of branch points. Our requirement that some basic Q-functions have a single cut on a certain Riemann sheet mimics the equivalent property of the AdS
5
/CFT
4
quantum spectral curve. But we also take an alternative point of view on this property: we give a simple example of how branch points can be consistently introduced into an integrable model in principle and we do not an attempt to define the most general Q-system with branch points.

Property 1 obviously favours the upper half-plane to the lower half-plane. And Property 2 makes preference for the Q-basis compared to the Hodge-dual one as
Q_{a|\emptyset},Q_{\emptyset|i}
have one cut while their Hodge-dual counterparts
Q^{a|\emptyset},Q^{\emptyset|i}
are only UHPA. It is natural to expect that these asymmetries are artefacts of a basis and a gauge choice. We reflect this expectation in the following two requirements:

Property 3:

It is possible to apply continuous symmetry transformations to get such a Q-system that its Hodge-dual satisfies properties 1 and 2.

Property 4:

It is possible to apply symmetry transformations to get a Q-system with the same properties 1-3 but valid in the lower half-plane.

3.3
Hodge-dual system.

Let us denote Hodge-dual Q-functions that satisfy properties 1,2 by calligraphic
\mathcal{Q}^{A|I}
and, in particular, we will use
{\bf P}^{a}\equiv\mathcal{Q}^{a|\emptyset}
,
{\bf Q}^{i}\equiv\mathcal{Q}^{\emptyset|i}
. We stress that
\mathcal{Q}^{A|I}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}
are not related just by (
5
) but by (
5
)
and
, in principle, appropriate H-rotations/gauge transformations. However, one can simplify the case: As both Q-systems are meromorphic in the upper half-plane, the H-rotations relating them should be also meromorphic there and, since H-matrices are
i
-periodic, they should be meromorphic everywhere. Therefore, without loss of generality, we can always choose such a basis in which no H-rotations are needed to relate
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{A|I}
.

We therefore need to consider only gauge transformations which will be written in the following parameterisation: the first transformation is
g_{b}=g_{f}=g
, the second transformation is given by function
h
, see (
19
). Sample relations between
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{A|I}
include
1=\mathcal{Q}^{\emptyset|\emptyset}=g^{2}\mathcal{Q}_{12|12}
,
{\bf P}^{a}\equiv\mathcal{Q}^{a|\emptyset}=\frac{g^{+}\,g^{-}}{h}\epsilon^{ab}\mathcal{Q}_{b|12}
.

We can represent Hasse diagram for the basis
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}
as follows:

(21)

In this representation, we favoured using functions
{\bf P},{\bf Q}
with both upper- and lower-indices because they have the simplest possible analytic properties.

3.4
Connecting upper and lower half-planes

We need to clarify what it entails to use symmetries in the context of Property 4. Because of the branch points, the Q-system is not uniquely defined in the lower half-plane. Generically, one expects infinitely many branch points below the real axis. One can see this phenomenon by solving (
1
) which becomes in the
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}
basis

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{+}-\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{-}={\bf P}_{a}\,{\bf Q}_{i}\,.

(22)

The solution analytic in the upper half-plane is the sum
\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}=-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left({\bf P}_{a}\,{\bf Q}_{i}\right)^{[2n+1]}
. It might need regularisation but the only thing we need to infer from the sum is that
\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}
has branch points at
u=\pm{\mbox{h}}-\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2}+\mathtt{i}\,\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
.

There are two main ways to deal with this ladder of branch points: to connect each pair of them by short cuts and then avoid these short cuts while performing analytic continuations (that is use the physical kinematics) or to connect each pair by long cuts (that is to use the mirror kinematics). We want that Property 4 holds for symmetry transformations respecting either mirror or physical kinematics. Choosing kinematics in this non-local set up is analogous to deciding whether to analytically continue while bypassing the branch point from the right or from the left (the notion which is well-defined only for local equations).

By Property 4, there is a Q-system with analyticity in the lower half-plane. Denote such system by
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}^{\downarrow}
and its analytic counterpart in the Hodge-dual basis as
\mathcal{Q}^{A|I}_{\downarrow}
. They are related by a gauge transformation, analogously to the transformation between
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{A|I}
. The corresponding gauge functions are denoted as
g_{\downarrow}
and
h_{\downarrow}
. For the future convenience we will introduce the following short-hand notation (with
\delta^{IJ}
,
\delta_{IJ}
being the multi-index delta-functions)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{A}|^{I}\equiv\delta^{IJ}\,\mathcal{Q}_{A|J}^{\downarrow}\,,\quad\mathcal{Q}^{A}|_{I}\equiv\delta_{IJ}\,\mathcal{Q}^{A|J}_{\downarrow}\,,

(23a)

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{a}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{Q}_{a}|^{\emptyset}\,,

\displaystyle{\bf P}^{a}_{\downarrow}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{Q}^{a}|_{\emptyset}\,,

\displaystyle{\bf Q}^{i}_{\downarrow}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{Q}_{\emptyset}|^{i}\,,

\displaystyle{\bf Q}_{i}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{Q}^{\emptyset}|_{i}=\epsilon_{ij}\mathcal{Q}_{12}|^{j}h_{\downarrow}\,g^{+}_{\downarrow}\,g^{-}_{\downarrow}\,.

(23b)

Hasse diagram for the basis
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}^{\downarrow}
is

(24)

All functions that appear on the diagram are LHPA (either it is the only natural choice like for
\mathcal{Q}_{a}|^{i}
or the default that we agree on like for
{\bf P}_{a}^{\downarrow})
.

Let us focus on the physical kinematics. Property 4 tells us that the bases
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}^{\downarrow}
are related, as functions in physical kinematics, by a combination of symmetry transformations. We can skip considering Hodge duality for relating these bases because if it is present we just change the labelling
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}^{\downarrow}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{Q}^{A|I}_{\downarrow}
. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{\emptyset|\emptyset}=\mathcal{Q}_{\emptyset|\emptyset}^{\downarrow}=1
, only one gauge transformation (
19
) is allowed; we parameterise this gauge transformation by the function
{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}
. Finally, one should consider H-rotations. One has no need to perform bosonic H-rotations because
{\bf P}_{a}
and
{\bf P}_{a}^{\downarrow}
are both analytic everywhere but on the cut
[-{\mbox{h}},{\mbox{h}}]
. Hence bosonic H-rotations are always meromorphic
12
12

12

Precise reasoning is the following: Let
{\bf P}^{\downarrow}_{a}={\hat{\mathsf{h}}}\,(H_{b})_{a}{}^{b}{\bf P}_{b}
and hence
\epsilon^{ab}{\bf P}^{\downarrow\,[2n]}_{a}{\bf P}^{\downarrow\,[2m]}_{b}={\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2n]}{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2m]}\det H_{b}\,\epsilon^{ab}{\bf P}^{[2n]}_{a}{\bf P}^{[2m]}_{b}
. Then
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2n]}{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2m]}}\vphantom{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2n]}{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2m]}}\det\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{H}\vphantom{H}_{b}={\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2n]}{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2m]}\det H_{b}
for any
n,m\neq 0
, which is only possible if
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2n]}}\vphantom{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2n]}}/{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2n]}
is the same for any
n\neq 0
. There is always exist a periodic function
\Upsilon
in the physical kinematics such that
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2]}}\vphantom{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2]}}/{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{[2]}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\Upsilon}\vphantom{\Upsilon}/\Upsilon
. Recall that periodic gauge transformations (
19
) with periodic
h
can be viewed as H-rotations, hence we can redefine
{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}:={\hat{\mathsf{h}}}/\Upsilon
,
H_{b}:=\Upsilon\,H_{b}
(this also changes
H_{f}
but the latter was not constrained by any means so far). After this rescaling,
{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}
has only cut
[-{\mbox{h}},{\mbox{h}}]
in the physical kinematics and hence
((H_{b})_{a}{}^{b}-(\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{H}\vphantom{H}_{b})_{a}{}^{b}){\bf P}_{b}^{[2n]}
, for any
n\neq 0
. We conclude that
H_{b}
cannot have branch points.
and one can redefine
e.g.
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}^{\downarrow}
to not consider them at all. The fermionic H-rotations are however non-trivial. We denote them by
\omega^{ji}\equiv\delta^{ik}(H_{f})_{k}{}^{j}
.
\omega
is an
i
-periodic
2\times 2
matrix in the physical kinematics. It does have an infinite ladder of short cuts and this is the matrix which transforms the semi-infinite ladder of cuts of
{\bf Q}_{i}
in the lower half-plane to the semi-infinite ladder of cuts of
{\bf Q}^{i}_{\downarrow}
in the upper half-plane. We can summarise the symmetry transformations as follows

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{a}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle={\hat{\mathsf{h}}}\,{\bf P}_{a}\,,

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{\downarrow}^{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}}{\bf P}^{a}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times\frac{h}{h_{\downarrow}}(-1)^{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}\,,

(25a)

\displaystyle{\bf Q}_{i}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle={\hat{\mathsf{h}}}\,\omega_{ij}\,{\bf Q}^{j}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times\frac{h_{\downarrow}}{h}\times(-1)^{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}\,,

\displaystyle{\bf Q}_{\downarrow}^{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}}\,{\bf Q}_{j}\,\omega^{ji}\,,

(25b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{a}|^{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{a|j}(\omega^{ji})^{\pm}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{g_{\downarrow}}{g}

\displaystyle=\omega^{+}\,.

(25c)

The factors written in
gray
can be ignored by the reader: we shall eventually conclude that
h,h_{\downarrow}
can be re-absorbed into Q-functions by the appropriate re-definitions, and
p_{\omega}
is set to
p_{\omega}=0
for the explicit examples that we study. Appearance of these factors and other aspects of the derivation of (
25
) shall be now clarified.

First of all, we defined

\displaystyle\omega^{2}\equiv\det\limits_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}\omega_{ij}\,\ \ \ \ \ \omega_{ij}\omega^{jk}=\delta_{i}{}^{k}\,.

(26)

The second relation in (
25c
) follows from (
16
). As only
\omega^{2}
is defined by (
26
), we can at will choose the sign when taking the square root, and we choose the one to get (
25c
) as written. However
\omega
can be either periodic or anti-periodic despite the matrix
\omega_{ij}
is periodic:
(\omega^{+})^{2}=(\omega^{-})^{2}
implies
\omega^{+}=(-1)^{{\rm p}_{\omega}}\omega^{-}
,
{\rm p}_{\omega}=0,1

13
13

13

This effect is important for the AdS
4
/CFT
3
QSC where
\mu_{AB}=\nu^{a}(\sigma_{AB})_{a}{}^{b}\nu_{b}
with
\nu^{a},\,\nu_{a}
Weyl spinors and
\sigma_{AB}
an anti-symmetrised product of gamma-matrices. Here
A,B=1,\dots,6
label components of a
\mathsf{SO}(6)
vector and
a,b=1,\dots,4
components of a spinor, they are not related to the indices used in the rest of the paper. While
\mu_{AB}
must be (mirror)-periodic, the spinors need only satisfy
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\nu}\vphantom{\nu}_{a}=e^{\mathtt{i}\mathcal{P}}\nu^{[2]}_{a}\,,\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\nu}\vphantom{\nu}^{a}=e^{-\mathtt{i}\mathcal{P}}(\nu^{a})^{[2]}
with
e^{\mathtt{i}\mathcal{P}}
being a state-dependent phase
Anselmetti:2015mda
;
Bombardelli:2017vhk
.

Furthermore, the full relation between
{\bf P}_{\downarrow}^{a}
and
{\bf P}^{a}
should read
{\bf P}_{\downarrow}^{a}=\frac{1}{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}}\frac{h}{h_{\downarrow}}\frac{g_{\downarrow}^{+}g_{\downarrow}^{-}}{g^{+}\,g^{-}}\frac{1}{\omega^{2}}{\bf P}^{a}
, but now we are aware that
\frac{g_{\downarrow}^{+}g_{\downarrow}^{-}}{g^{+}\,g^{-}}\frac{1}{\omega^{2}}=(-1)^{{\rm p}_{\omega}}
, hence
{\bf P}_{\downarrow}^{a}=\frac{1}{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}}\frac{h}{h_{\downarrow}}{\bf P}^{a}\times(-1)^{{\rm p}_{\omega}}
. As
{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}
has only one short cut
[-{\mbox{h}},{\mbox{h}}]
,
\frac{h}{h_{\downarrow}}
has also only one short cut. Furthermore,
h
is UHPA and
h_{\downarrow}
is LHPA, and so
h
,
h_{\downarrow}
each have only one short cut in the physical kinematics.

In the following we will consider transformations in the mirror kinematics which imply, following a similar reasoning, that
h,h_{\downarrow}
have only one long cut in the mirror kinematics. Therefore we can perform, without spoiling the cut structure, a gauge transformation on
\mathcal{Q}^{A|J}
to absorb
h
(thus effectively redefining
{\bf Q}^{i},{\bf P}^{a}
) and a gauge transformation on
\mathcal{Q}^{A|J}_{\downarrow}
to absorb
h_{\downarrow}
. In this adjusted gauge
h=1
,
h_{\downarrow}=1
. We shall however keep writing
h/h_{\downarrow}
until the relations in the mirror kinematics are introduced explicitly to avoid a potential loop in the logic.

3.5
Monodromy bootstrap

The construction in the last subsection gave us an explicit realisation of Property 4. There is a fermionic H-rotation
\omega
supplied by a gauge transformation
{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}
which relate, through the physical kinematics,
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}^{\downarrow}
– two bases analytic in the different half-planes. We can formally denote this relation as
\mathcal{Q}^{\downarrow}=\omega_{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}}\cdot\mathcal{Q}
.

As discussed, we do not intend to give any preference to the physical kinematics, and so an equivalent relation in the mirror kinematics should exist. Now one should have a bosonic H-rotation (let us call it
\mu
) and a gauge transformation
{\check{\mathsf{h}}}
. The question is: what is the relation between
\mathcal{Q}^{\downarrow}
and
\mu_{{\check{\mathsf{h}}}}\cdot\mathcal{Q}
? We demand that they are related by a symmetry transformation because these two Q-systems analytic in the lower half-plane describe the same physical system. As all the gauge transformations and rotations can be absorbed into
\omega_{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}
and
\mu_{\check{\mathsf{h}}}
after proper adjustments, there are only two conceptual possibilities. Choosing and implementing one of them severely constraints the Q-system, this is the key feature of the monodromy bootstrap idea.

The first option is:

Crossing equation A

\displaystyle\mu_{\check{\mathsf{h}}}\cdot\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}^{\downarrow}=\omega_{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}\cdot\mathcal{Q}\ \ \ \ \leftrightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \mathcal{Q}=(\mu_{\check{\mathsf{h}}})^{-1}\cdot(\omega_{\hat{\mathsf{h}}})\cdot\mathcal{Q}\,.

(27)

This means that by either going through physical or going through mirror we arrive at the same Q-system, after appropriate symmetry adjustments.

Alternatively, we can think about performing a specific-type monodromy procedure ‘around the branch point
u={\mbox{h}}
’ which we symbolically denote

\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{Q}\vphantom{Q}
and then the crossing equation above reads
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{Q}\vphantom{Q}\simeq Q
, where
\simeq
means ‘up to continuous symmetries’. We emphasise that
Q
stands here for the whole Q-system and then
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{Q}\vphantom{Q}
is not simply an analytic continuation: We are changing the way to describe Q-system while moving along the contour to resolve the conflict of non-local equations being continued around branch points, see Fig.
1
.

Figure 1:
Analytic continuation perspective on the monodromy bootstrap. Crosses represent the Q-system, there are two them to emphasise the non-locality of the equations. Q-system can be parameterised using the physical or the mirror kinematics, and an UHPA or a LHPA basis. Along the contour of continuation
abcd
, only two out of four descriptions are suitable for each of the segments
a,b,c,d
. For instance, while on
a
, we should use an UHPA basis but the kinematics is irrelevant; while on
b
, we should use the physical kinematics and either an UHPA or a LHPA basis are acceptable. Before moving from a segment to the next one, we should use a continuous symmetry transformation to switch to the description suitable for both segments.

The second possibility is

Crossing equation B

\displaystyle\mu_{\check{\mathsf{h}}}\cdot\mathcal{Q}^{*}=\mathcal{Q}^{\downarrow}=\omega_{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}\cdot\mathcal{Q}\ \ \ \ \leftrightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \mathcal{Q}^{*}=(\mu_{\check{\mathsf{h}}})^{-1}\cdot(\omega_{\hat{\mathsf{h}}})\cdot\mathcal{Q}\,,

(28)

where
∗
means taking the Hodge dual.

Using the above-described monodromy procedure, crossing equation B can be symbolically denoted as
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{Q}\vphantom{Q}\simeq Q^{*}
.

In this paper we focus on the detailed presentation of the B-case because it will eventually lead to Hubbard model. Case A although physically different can be studied in full analogy, we shall summarise its main properties in Section
5
and give further clarifications in Appendix
A
.

Explicit realisation of
\mu_{\check{\mathsf{h}}}\cdot\mathcal{Q}^{*}=\mathcal{Q}^{\downarrow}
is

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{a}^{\downarrow}={{\check{\mathsf{h}}}}\,\mu_{ab}\,{\bf P}^{b}\,,

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{\downarrow}^{a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{{\check{\mathsf{h}}}}\,{\bf P}_{b}\,\mu^{ba}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times\frac{h}{h_{\downarrow}}\times(-1)^{{\rm p}_{\mu}}}\,,

(29a)

\displaystyle{\bf Q}^{i}_{\downarrow}=\frac{1}{{\check{\mathsf{h}}}}\,{\bf Q}^{i}\,,

\displaystyle{\bf Q}^{\downarrow}_{i}

\displaystyle=-{{\check{\mathsf{h}}}}\,{\bf Q}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times\frac{h_{\downarrow}}{h}\times(-1)^{{\rm p}_{\mu}}}\,,

(29b)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{Q}_{a}|^{i}&=\mu_{ab}^{\pm}\mathcal{Q}^{b|i}\,,\\
\mathcal{Q}^{a}|_{i}&=\mathcal{Q}_{b|i}(\mu^{ba})^{\pm}\,,\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\mu^{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{g_{\downarrow}}{g}}\,,

(29c)

where

\displaystyle\mu^{2}\equiv\det\limits_{1\leq a,b\leq 2}\mu_{ab}\,,\ \ \ \ \ \mu_{ac}\mu^{cb}=\delta_{a}{}^{b}\,.

(30)

The derivation of (
29
) is analogous to the one for (
25
), with
(-1)^{{\rm p}_{\mu}}\equiv\mu^{+}/\mu^{-}
in the mirror kinematics. At this stage we confirm that
h
and
h_{\downarrow}
are, respectively, UHPA and LHPA functions both having one short cut in the physical kinematics and one long cut in the mirror kinematics; hence, as announced, they can be removed by a gauge transformation without spoiling the analytic properties. From now on we won’t write them any longer.

4
Exploring analytic properties

We shall now explore explicit consequences of implementing the monodromy bootstrap requirement.

4.1
Function
F
and
\mathsf{PSU}
vs
\mathsf{SU}
cases

Consider equations
\frac{g_{\downarrow}}{g}=\omega^{+}
,
\frac{1}{g_{\downarrow}\,g}=\mu^{+}
. From the periodicity of
\omega
in the physical kinematics we conclude

\displaystyle\frac{g_{\downarrow}^{+}}{g_{\downarrow}^{-}}={(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}\frac{g^{+}}{g^{-}}\,,

(31)

and from the periodicity of
\mu
in the mirror kinematics we conclude

\displaystyle\frac{g_{\downarrow}^{-}}{g_{\downarrow}^{+}}={(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}}}\frac{g^{+}}{g^{-}}\,.

(32)

Recall that
g_{\downarrow}
is LHPA which has no cuts for
\Im(u)<1/2
and
g
is UHPA which has no cuts for
\Im(u)>1/2
. Then
F\equiv\frac{g^{+}}{g^{-}}
is very special as we conclude from the above relations that
F
is both UHPA and LHPA and, considered as either, has only one cut in both the physical and the mirror kinematics. This is only possible if
F
is a single-valued function of variable
x
(
20
), and moreover from (
31
) and (
32
) we derive

\displaystyle F(x)F(1/x)={(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\omega}}+{{\rm p}_{\mu}}}}\,.

(33)

Indeed, consider
F
in the upper half-plane and continue it, using the physical kinematics, to the lower half-plane where it can be computed as
{(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}}}\frac{g_{\downarrow}^{-}}{g_{\downarrow}^{+}}
; then continue it to the upper half-plane using the mirror kinematics and use (
31
).

For definiteness, we shall treat
F
as UHPA. Introduce an UHPA function
f
and an LHPA function
\bar{f}
using the relations

\displaystyle\ g=\frac{1}{f^{+}}\,\ \ g_{\downarrow}=\bar{f}^{-}\,.

(34)

The meaning of
f,\bar{f}
is that they solve

\displaystyle\frac{f}{f^{[2]}}=\frac{\bar{f}}{\bar{f}^{[-2]}}=F\,,

(35)

a formal solution with the required analyticity properties is

f=\prod\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}F(x^{[2n]})\,,\ \bar{f}=\prod\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}F(x^{[-2n]})\,.
While it may need regularisation, it gives the right description of the cut structure of
f,\bar{f}
and hence of
\mu,\omega
that can be computed as

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\,{\bar{f}}^{[-2]}=f^{[2]}\,{\bar{f}}=f^{[2]}\,F\,{\bar{f}}^{[-2]}=\prod_{n=-\infty}^{+\infty}F^{[2n]}\,,

(36a)

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{f}{{\bar{f}}^{[-2]}}=F\,\frac{f^{[2]}}{{\bar{f}}^{[-2]}}=F\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}F^{[2n]}/F^{[-2n]}\,.

(36b)

We recall that this equation is in the physical kinematics, its version in the mirror kinematics reads

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle={f}\,{\bar{f}}^{[-2]}={\rm C}\times F\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}F^{[2n]}/F^{[-2n]}\,,

(37)

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=\frac{f}{{\bar{f}}^{[-2]}}={\rm C}\times\prod_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}F^{[2n]}\,,

(38)

where the value of constant
{\rm C}
depends on how the product is regularised. Of course, we should be only interested in such expressions where
{\rm C}
and regulators cancel out.

It is useful to note the following simple consequence of the above formulae

\displaystyle\frac{\mu}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}}=\frac{\omega}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\omega}\vphantom{\omega}}=\frac{F}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{F}\vphantom{F}}=F^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\omega}}+{{\rm p}_{\mu}}}}\,,

(39)

and rewrite QQ-relatios (
1
) using
\mathcal{Q},{\bf P},{\bf Q}
and
F

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{F}\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{-}{\bf Q}^{i}={F}\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{+}{\bf Q}^{i}\,,

(40a)

\displaystyle{\bf Q}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{F}\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{-}{\bf P}^{a}={F}\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{+}{\bf P}^{a}\,,

(40b)

\displaystyle{\bf P}^{a}

\displaystyle=-F(\mathcal{Q}^{a|i})^{-}{\bf Q}_{i}=-\frac{1}{F}(\mathcal{Q}^{a|i})^{+}{\bf Q}_{i}\,,

(40c)

\displaystyle{\bf Q}^{i}

\displaystyle=-F(\mathcal{Q}^{a|i})^{-}{\bf P}_{a}=-\frac{1}{F}(\mathcal{Q}^{a|i})^{+}{\bf P}_{a}\,,

(40d)

alongside with

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}\mathcal{Q}^{a|j}=-\delta_{i}{}^{j}\,,\quad\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}\mathcal{Q}^{b|i}=-\delta_{a}{}^{b}.

(41)

Probably the most important conclusion is that (
1
) becomes

\displaystyle{\bf P}^{a}{\bf P}_{a}={\bf Q}^{i}{\bf Q}_{i}=\frac{1}{F}-F\,.

(42)

For the AdS
5
/CFT
4
QSC, the corresponding relation is
{\bf P}^{a}{\bf P}_{a}={\bf Q}^{i}{\bf Q}_{i}=0
meaning that
F^{2}=1
. This choice of the value for
F
is of course very special, we shall refer to it as the zero central charge condition. Implementing it means that we are dealing with
\mathsf{PSU}
but not
\mathsf{SU}
systems. The intuition is based on two observations: First, in the case of spin chains without branch points, it is known that
Q_{\bar{\emptyset}|\bar{\emptyset}}^{+}/Q_{\bar{\emptyset}|\bar{\emptyset}}^{-}
is related to and, for an appropriate choice of scalings, is equal to quantum determinant
14
14

14

Using the same notations as in our paper, a derivation is available in
Chernyak:2020lgw
and is hinted in
Gromov:2010km
; the statement itself is known for a long time under different disguises. Indeed, if one reformulates it using Drinfeld polynomials, it reduces to basic facts from representation theory of Yangians/quantum affine algebras.
. Second, in the AdS
5
/CFT
4
case we are dealing with
\mathsf{PSU}(2,2|4)
system.

Let us analyse what
F^{2}=1
implies for Q-functions:
g^{2}
should be periodic and hence analytic everywhere. Since
{\bf P}^{a}{\bf P}_{a}={\bf Q}^{i}{\bf Q}_{i}=0
, there exist functions
\alpha,\bar{\alpha}
such that
{\bf P}_{a}=\alpha\,\epsilon_{ab}{\bf P}^{a}
,
{\bf Q}_{i}=\bar{\alpha}\,\epsilon_{ij}{\bf Q}^{j}
. On the other hand

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{1}\,{\bf Q}_{1}=\mathcal{Q}_{1|1}^{+}-\mathcal{Q}_{1|1}^{-}=-\frac{1}{(g^{2})^{+}}((\mathcal{Q}^{2|2})^{+}-(\mathcal{Q}^{2|2})^{-})=-\frac{1}{(g^{2})^{+}}{\bf P}^{2}\,{\bf Q}^{2}\,.

(43)

Therefore
\bar{\alpha}=-(g^{2})^{+}\frac{1}{\alpha}
. Being a ratio of
{\bf P}
,
\alpha
is an UHPA with only one cut in the physical kinematics. Similarly
\bar{\alpha}
is an UHPA with only one cut in the mirror kinematics. And hence, since
g
is analytic,
\alpha,\bar{\alpha}
have only one cut in both the physical and the mirror kinematics. We hence can absorb
\alpha,\bar{\alpha}
by a gauge transformation to
{\bf P}^{a},{\bf Q}^{i}
.

In conclusion, in the zero central charge case there exist a gauge in which Hasse diagram looks as

(44)

that is, in the worst case up to a rescaling with a periodic function
g
, Q-system and its Hodge dual are identical. In particular, the A-case and the B-case of Q-systems are the same.

In this paper, we do not, as a rule, assume
\frac{1}{F}-F=0
because some interesting physics will be missed out otherwise, this is one of major distinctions compared to AdS
5
/CFT
4
. But of course, zero central charge cases will be also analysed.

4.2
Discontinuity relations for
{\bf P}
and
{\bf Q}

We now proceed with systematic elimination of LHPA functions in order to get a closed set of equations on
{\bf P},\mu
and
{\bf Q},\omega
.

First, we derive the monodromy properties of
{\bf P}
and
{\bf Q}
. Let us explain how it is done on the example of
{\bf P}_{a}
. Start in the upper half-plane and continue to the lower half-plane using the physical kinematics, then we can use
{\bf P}_{a}=\frac{1}{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}}{\bf P}_{a}^{\downarrow}
. In the lower half-plane we can switch to the mirror kinematics using the fact that
{\bf P}_{a}^{\downarrow}
is LHPA. We continue then up to the upper half-plane and use, in the mirror kinematics,
{\bf P}_{a}^{\downarrow}={\check{\mathsf{h}}}\,\mu_{ab}{\bf P}^{b}
. In summary, we performed a nontrivial analytic continuation of
{\bf P}_{a}
around the branch point and concluded that the result is given by
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}={\check{\mathsf{h}}}/{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}}\vphantom{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}}}\mu_{ab}{\bf P}^{b}
.

In the described procedure
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}
is the
clock-wise
analytic continuation (if we focus on the right branch point). But we can also first go down through the mirror kinematics and then go back through the physical kinematics. In the result of this procedure we achieve the
counter-clock-wise
analytic continuation
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}
.

Define

\displaystyle r\equiv\frac{{\check{\mathsf{h}}}}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}}\vphantom{{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}}}\,.

(45)

The monodromy data for
{\bf P}
and
{\bf Q}
derived in the above-described way is summarised below. The equations are valid as written for
0<\Im(u)<1
.

Clock-wise

Counterclock-wise

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}

\displaystyle=r\,\mu_{ab}{\bf P}^{b}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}

\displaystyle=-\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}\,{\bf P}^{b}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}}_{ba}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}\,,

(46a)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{r}\,{\bf P}_{b}\,\mu^{ba}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}}}\,{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}}^{ab}{\bf P}_{b}\,,

(46b)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf Q}}\vphantom{{\bf Q}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\,{\bf Q}^{j}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\omega}\vphantom{\omega}}_{ji}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf Q}}\vphantom{{\bf Q}}_{i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}}\,\omega_{ij}{\bf Q}^{j}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}\,,

(46c)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf Q}}\vphantom{{\bf Q}}^{i}

\displaystyle=-r\,{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\omega}\vphantom{\omega}}^{ij}{\bf Q}_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf Q}}\vphantom{{\bf Q}}^{i}

\displaystyle={\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}}\,{\bf Q}_{j}\,\omega^{ji}\,.

(46d)

4.3
Discontinuity relations for
\mu_{ab}
and
\omega_{ij}

First we notice that
\mu_{ab}
and
\omega_{ij}
can be related in a straightforward way. Indeed, take the first equation in (
25c
) and (
29c
) and eliminate
\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{\downarrow}
. One gets then using (
41
)

\displaystyle\mu_{ab}=-\omega^{ij}\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{-}\mathcal{Q}_{b|j}^{-}\,,

(47a)

\displaystyle\omega^{ij}=-\mu_{ab}(\mathcal{Q}^{a|i}\mathcal{Q}^{b|j})^{-}\,,

(47b)

\displaystyle\omega_{ij}=-\mu^{ab}\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{-}\mathcal{Q}_{b|j}^{-}\,,

(47c)

\displaystyle\mu^{ab}=-\omega_{ij}(\mathcal{Q}^{a|i}\mathcal{Q}^{b|j})^{-}\,.

(47d)

In the following we shall mostly focus on the properties of
\mu
only sparsely mentioning
\omega
. The properties of the latter can be derived from (
47
).

Assume for the moment that
\mu_{ab}
and hence
\omega_{ij}
have non-vanishing anti-symmetric parts. Then it follows from (
47
) that
\det\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{-}=-\frac{1}{4}{\epsilon^{ab}\mu_{ab}}\,{\epsilon^{ij}\omega_{ij}}
. On the other hand, we can also compute
\left(\det\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{-}\right)^{2}={\mu}^{2}\,{\omega}^{2}
and thus conclude that
\frac{(\frac{1}{2}{\epsilon^{ab}\mu_{ab}})^{2}}{\mu^{2}}=\frac{\omega^{2}}{(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ij}\omega_{ij})^{2}}
. The l.h.s. is periodic in the mirror kinematics while the r.h.s. is periodic in the physical kinematics which implies that both sides are periodic functions without cuts. We shall assume that taking a square root does not introduce Zhukovsky-type branch points
15
15

15

This is the
\sqrt{}
-assumption used explicitly or implicitly in several places across the paper, we discuss it in Appendix
B
.
, and then
\frac{\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ab}\mu_{ab}}{\mu}=\pm\frac{\omega}{\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ij}\omega_{ij}}
is a function without such branch points. We do not know whether it is periodic or anti-periodic, however we can conclude from this exercise that

\displaystyle{(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}=1\,.

(48)

We cannot perform this argumentation for a potentially possible case
\mu_{ab}=\mu_{ba}
. This is one of the reasons to keep the factor
{(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}
in the formulae. Also, the presented derivation of case B is a basis for a derivation of the later-defined case C
16
16

16

The derivation itself for case C is absent from the paper. One needs to simply put bars in the correct places of the case-B derivation, the conventions are introduced by (
68
), (
114
), (
115
).
, where the logic to cancel similar factors is slightly altered, as explained after (
68
).

An important monodromy property of
\mu_{ab}
comes from its periodicity in the mirror kinematics. When
\mu_{ab}
is considered with short cuts, this periodicity transforms into the relation
Gromov:2013pga

\displaystyle{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}}_{ab}=\mu_{ab}^{[2]}\,.

(49)

An analogous relation for
\omega_{ij}
exists as an equation in the mirror kinematics:
{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\omega}\vphantom{\omega}}_{ij}=\omega_{ij}^{[2]}
.

Consider now the l.h.s. equations in (
29c
) and note that
(\mathcal{Q}_{a}|^{i})^{-}
has no cut on the real axis, therefore
\Delta(\mu_{ab}(\mathcal{Q}^{b|i})^{-})=0
, where
\Delta
stands for the discontinuity. Then use
\Delta(\mu_{ab}(\mathcal{Q}^{b|i})^{-})=(\mathcal{Q}^{b|i})^{+}\Delta(\mu_{ab})-\Delta(\mu_{ab}{\bf P}^{b}{\bf Q}^{i})
from where we conclude

\displaystyle\Delta(\mu_{ab})=-\Delta(\frac{1}{F}\mu_{ac}\,{\bf P}^{c}\,{\bf P}_{b})=-\Delta(\frac{1}{F\,r}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}\,{\bf P}_{b})\,.

(50)

As we departed from
\Delta(\ldots)=0
, we can choose both
\Delta(f)=f-\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{f}\vphantom{f}
and
\Delta(f)=f-\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{f}\vphantom{f}
. The first choice yields

\displaystyle\mu_{ab}-{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}}_{ab}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{F\,r}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}}_{a}{\bf P}_{b}+\frac{1}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{F}\vphantom{F}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\approx$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}}_{a}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{b}

(51a)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{F}\mu_{ac}{\bf P}^{c}{\bf P}_{b}-F\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ac}\mu^{dc}{\bf P}_{d}{\bf P}^{e}\mu_{be}\,,

(51b)

whereas the second one yields

\displaystyle\mu_{ab}-{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}}_{ab}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{F\,r}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}}_{a}{\bf P}_{b}+\frac{1}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{F}\vphantom{F}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}}{\bf P}_{a}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{b}

(52a)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{F}\mu_{ac}{\bf P}^{c}{\bf P}_{b}-F{\bf P}_{a}{\bf P}^{c}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{cb}\,.

(52b)

The second option is clearly a more concise expression and we can easily spot the reason:
f-\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{f}\vphantom{f}
is the discontinuity across the long cut which is preferable to be used for functions with simpler mirror kinematics, such as
\mu_{ab}
.

4.4
The most general
{\bf P}\mu
-system

We can explicitly solve (
52b
) for
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ab}

\displaystyle\mu_{ab}^{[2]}=\left(\delta_{a}^{c}+\frac{1}{F}{\bf P}_{a}\,{\bf P}^{c}\right)\mu_{cd}\left(\delta_{b}^{d}+\frac{1}{F}{\bf P}^{d}\,{\bf P}_{b}\right)\,.

(53)

Above we wrote the solution, using (
49
), as the finite difference equation (an analog of Baxter equation), but we also can represent it as an explicit Riemann-Hilbert problem

\displaystyle\mu_{ab}(u-\mathtt{i}0)=\left(\delta_{a}^{c}+\frac{1}{F}{\bf P}_{a}\,{\bf P}^{c}\right)\,\mu_{cd}(u+\mathtt{i}0)\,\left(\delta_{b}^{d}+\frac{1}{F}{\bf P}^{d}\,{\bf P}_{b}\right)\,,\quad\Im(u)=0\,,\,|\Re(u)|>{\mbox{h}}\,.

(54)

We wrote this expression as the discontinuity relation on the long cut and for this sake, contrary to the typical practice across the paper, we mixed the two kinematics:
\mu
is considered in the mirror kinematics and
{\bf P},F
are considered in the physical kinematics.

Observe that the obtained relation depends only on the bilinears
{\bf P}_{a}\,{\bf P}^{b}
of functions
{\bf P}
which is suggestive of writing the discontinuity across the short Zhukovsky cut directly for these bilinears:

\displaystyle({\bf P}_{a}\,{\bf P}^{b})(u-\mathtt{i}\,0)=-\mu_{ac}({\bf P}^{c}{\bf P}_{d})(u+\mathtt{i}\,0)\,\mu^{db}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\omega}}+{{\rm p}_{\mu}}}}\,,\quad-{\mbox{h}}<u<{\mbox{h}}\,.

(55)

Here again
\mu
is taken in the mirror kinematics and
{\bf P}
are in the physical kinematics.

To complete the system, we rewrite (
33
) as

\displaystyle F(u+\mathtt{i}\,0)F(u-\mathtt{i}\,0)=1{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\omega}}+{{\rm p}_{\mu}}}}\,,\quad-{\mbox{h}}<u<{\mbox{h}}\,.

(56)

Together, equations (
54
),(
55
),(
56
) and
{\bf P}^{a}{\bf P}_{a}=\frac{1}{F}-F
form the closed system of equations –
{\bf P}\mu
-system or quantum spectral curve derived from the type-B monodromy bootstrap.

To get an explicit solution of the derived QSC, one should supplement it with input from physics such as asymptotic behaviour at infinity, structure of pole/zeros and similar. Then one can try applying ‘density on the cut’ strategy for finding solutions of Riemann-Hilbert problems, successful examples for other systems can be found
e.g.
in
Gromov:2008gj
;
Kazakov:2010kf
;
Gromov:2011cx
. We will not attempt executing a similar study for a general type-B QSC as this would be a research project on its own. Instead, we discuss some universal features of this QSC and then, in the next subsection and Section
6
, pick up a specific subcase for the detailed analysis.

First, we comment how to recover functions
{\bf Q},\omega
once
{\bf P},\mu,F
are known. The key observation are the following relations

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{+}-\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{-}={\bf P}_{a}{\bf Q}_{i}=\frac{1}{F}{\bf P}_{a}{\bf P}^{b}\mathcal{Q}_{b|i}^{-}

(57)

which can be considered as equations on
\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}
. There are two solutions, labelled by
i=1,2
. We fix
\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}
and then compute
{\bf Q}_{i},{\bf Q}^{i}
and
\omega_{ij}
using (
40
) and (
47
). We should also further verify that
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
and
\omega_{ij}
derived in this way indeed have the expected analytic properties as a consequence of equations on
{\bf P},\mu,F
. This exercise is straightforward and we do not present it here. An equivalent approach was already elaborated for AdS
5
/CFT
4
case in
Gromov:2014caa
, we refer to this paper for further clarifications.

Probably the most important novel feature is that the above-derived
{\bf P}\mu
-system generically requires more complicated branch point types than square roots, we formulate the corresponding no-go statements later on. Alongside with appearance of function
F
, this is a major qualitative distinction compared to the known examples of AdS
5
/CFT
4
and AdS
4
/CFT
3
QSC’s. Therefore, we won’t assume any particular type of branch points.

Nevertheless, some of the encountered functions or their combinations are forced to have square root cuts. We recall that
\det\mu
and
F
are examples of this type,
F
can even be uniformised by introduction of Zhukovsky variable. Furthermore, we notice that symmetry of (
53
) allows to consistently project
\mu_{ab}
to either symmetric or antisymmetric matrices. For the antisymmetrisation it is straightforward to derive

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{ab}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ab}}{\epsilon^{ab}\mu_{ab}}=\frac{1}{F^{2}}\,;

(58)

For symmetrisation, taking the determinant of (
53
) one concludes

\displaystyle\frac{\det\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{+}}{\det\mu_{+}}=\frac{1}{F^{4}}\,

(59)

meaning that branch points of
\epsilon^{ab}\mu_{ab}
and
\sqrt{\det\mu_{+}}
are of square-root type. As the full determinant is computed by
\det\mu=\det\mu_{+}+\det\mu_{-}
, there are only two independent combinations of four functions
\mu_{ab}
that are guaranteed to have square-root type branch points.

Combinations of type
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}\,{\bf P}
also have simpler properties. Derivation goes as follows

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{a}\mu^{ab}{\bf P}_{b}=-{\bf P}_{a}(\mathcal{Q}^{a|i}\mathcal{Q}^{b|j})^{-}{\bf P}_{b}\,\omega_{ij}=-\frac{1}{F^{4}}{\bf P}_{a}(\mathcal{Q}^{a|i}\mathcal{Q}^{b|j})^{+}{\bf P}_{b}\,\omega_{ij}=\frac{1}{F^{4}}{\bf P}_{a}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}^{ab}{\bf P}_{b}\,,

(60)

and then

\displaystyle\Upsilon\,\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{a}{\bf P}_{a}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{a}{\bf P}_{a}\,,\quad\,

(61a)

for
\Upsilon=-\frac{r}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}}F^{4}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\omega}}+{{\rm p}_{\mu}}}}
. In a similar way we derive

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Upsilon}\,{{\bf P}}^{a}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}={{\bf P}}^{a}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}\,

(61b)

and so

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}^{a}{\bf P}_{b}}\vphantom{{\bf P}^{a}{\bf P}_{b}}{\bf P}^{b}{\bf P}_{a}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}^{a}{\bf P}_{b}}\vphantom{{\bf P}^{a}{\bf P}_{b}}{\bf P}^{b}{\bf P}_{a}

(62)

is a function without branch points on the real axis.

Equations (
61
) have appearance of function
r
which we have no means to fix in this general set up. We note in particular that by doing gauge transformations we can redefine
r
by maps of type
r\to rh\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{h}\vphantom{h}
and, unless the branch points are of special type and/or other assumptions are supplied about the system, it is not easy to engineer an invariant under this transformation. On the other hand, all the observations we made and the experience with rational spin chains demonstrate that bilinears
{\bf P}_{a}{\bf P}^{b}
(and also
{\bf P}_{a}{\bf Q}_{i}
) are the only physically relevant combinations. Factor
r
never appears in this combinations, for instance it cancels from the product of (
61
) which is (
62
).

4.5
No-go theorem for square root cuts

Let us investigate what happens in the case when branch points are of square root type. Our first observation is

If
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-2.11113pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-2.11113pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}
then
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-2.11113pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ab}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-2.11113pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ab}
.

Indeed, (
61
) becomes
(1-\Upsilon)\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{a}{\bf P}_{a}=0
. Let us consider first the case
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{a}{\bf P}_{a}=0
which is equivalent to
{\bf P}_{a}\mu^{ab}{\bf P}_{b}=0
. Since
\mu_{ab}
is periodic in the mirror kinematics and
{\bf P}_{a}
is UHPA we conclude

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{a}^{[2n]}\mu^{ab}{\bf P}_{b}^{[2n]}=0\,\ \ \ \ n=1,2,\ldots\,.

(63)

It is safe to assume that
{\bf P}_{1}/{\bf P}_{2}
is not periodic (otherwise
e.g.
Q_{12|\emptyset}=0
) and then the last equation can be solved only by an antisymmetric matrix. If
\mu_{ab}
is antisymmetric, it has automatically branch points of square root type, cf. (
58
). If, on the other hand,
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{a}{\bf P}_{a}\neq 0
then
\Upsilon=1
which implies
