\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}
, then r.h.s. of (
51a
) and (
52a
) coincide implying
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ab}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ab}
.

In conclusion of this reasoning, we see that if
{\bf P}_{a}
have square root-type branch points then all functions have branch points of this type. We shall attempt therefore a seemingly weaker requirement that only
\mu_{ab}
have branch points of the square root type. It appears to be also very restrictive. We formulate it in the form of a

No-go theorem
:

If
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-2.11113pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ab}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-2.11113pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ab}
and
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-2.11113pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ab}\neq\mu_{ab}
then
\mu_{ab}=\mu\,\epsilon_{ab}
is the only possible structure of
\mu_{ab}
.

In particular, if
F=\pm 1
then from (
39
) we see that
\mu
has no cuts
17
17

17

p_{\mu}=0
because
\mu=\mu_{12}
, and for the same reason
p_{\omega}=0
.
. Hence, assuming the imposed Properties 1-4, QSC with zero central charge
cannot have
square root branch points.

In our proof of the no-go theorem we assume that
{\bf P}_{1},{\bf P}_{2},{\bf P}^{1},{\bf P}^{2}
, apart from constraint (
42
), have certain algebraic independence from one another. What this means exactly is clear in the proof, but we notice that it is sufficient to have Q-functions with (twisted) power-like large-
u
asymptotic and with
{\bf P}_{1}
and
{\bf P}_{2}
having different exponents. A power-like asymptotics is the most typical for AdS/CFT integrable systems. If dependence of
{\bf P}
on
u
is periodic like in
Klabbers:2017vtw
then the period should be not in a resonance with
\mathtt{i}
– period of
\mu
.

The idea of the proof is the following one: we can solve (
52b
) for
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ab}
giving it as a function of
\mu,{\bf P},F
. But because we assumed
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ab}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{ab}
, we can equate the obtained function to the r.h.s. of (
53
). As a result we get equation
H({\bf P}^{a}{\bf P}_{b},F,\mu_{ab})=0\,,
where
H
can be brought to a form polynomial in its arguments. Similarly to (
63
), infinitely many relations should be satisfied

\displaystyle H(({\bf P}^{a}{\bf P}_{b})^{[2n]},F^{[2n]},\mu_{ab})=0\,,\quad n=0,1,2,3\ldots\,.

(64)

This is very constraining of course and, by analysing the equation, one concludes that (
63
) is a way to satisfy (
64
) and that it is the only viable way.

Given the small size of the system, an exhaustive analysis of (
64
) can be done by brute force using symbolic programming, it is probably the fastest way to reach the conclusion. But to make things less mysterious, we also offer an explicit pen and paper analysis, it is presented in Appendix
B
.

We proceed now with the case
\mu_{ab}=\mu\,\epsilon_{ab}
. From (
55
) it is clear that
{\bf P}_{a}{\bf P}^{b}
have branch points of square root type in the discussed set up and hence we can expect that terms
{\bf P}_{a}
,
{\bf P}^{b}
have square-root branch points separately. This is confirmed by the existence of
r
consistent with this choice. Indeed, since
\mu_{11}=0
we also have
0=\Delta\big(\frac{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{1}{\bf P}_{1}}{F\,r}\big)
from (
50
) implying
F\,r=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{F}\vphantom{F}\,\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}
. We can then fix

\displaystyle r=\frac{1}{F}\,

(65)

up to an analytic function, and the analytic function can be absorbed into
{\bf P}^{a}
by a gauge transformation.

To be accurate, the above statement works only if
r
is known to not have branch points outside of the real axis. In reality though, we only know from the original derivation that
r
has no branch points in the lower half-plane of the mirror kinematics. We should additionally assume that
r
is free from cuts in the upper half-plane, while, to our understanding, we cannot derive this feature from the already postulated properties. We hence add it to the list of our assumptions. It is supported by the fact that the physical system has to obey reality conditions. But setting aside symmetries under complex conjugation, here is a different argumentation: Both functions
{\bf P}_{a}^{\downarrow}
and
{\bf P}_{a}
are analytic outside the cut
[-{\mbox{h}},{\mbox{h}}]
. However,
{\bf P}_{a}^{\downarrow}
can be used to construct the lower half-plane analytic system whereas
{\bf P}_{a}
generically cannot be used for this goal because
{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}^{-1}{\bf Q}_{i}^{\downarrow}
is not necessarily a function with only one long cut. Following our general idea that all Q-functions are equally good we impose the demand

Property 5

{\bf P}_{a}
can be used in construction of the lower half-plane analytic system and vice versa:
{\bf P}_{a}^{\downarrow}
can be used in construction of the upper half-plane system. The same property should hold for the pair
{\bf Q}_{i}
and
{\bf Q}_{i}^{\downarrow}
as well.

This implies that
{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}
and
{\check{\mathsf{h}}}
, as UHPA, should have only one short cut in the defining domain of the physical kinematics and one long cut in the defining domain of the mirror kinematics. The desired property of
r
follows.

5
All
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
and
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)\!\times\!\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
QSC’s fixed by the monodromy bootstrap

\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{Q}\vphantom{Q}\simeq Q^{*}

(B)

In the previous section we described in detail the discontinuity relations originating from the monodromy bootstrap condition
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{Q}\vphantom{Q}\simeq Q^{*}
. Let us now rewrite them in the index-free form. Denote
V=\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
V^{*}
its dual space, we shall think about
{\bf P}_{a}
as coordinates of vector
{\bf P}\in V
and
{\bf P}^{a}
as coordinates of the vector
{\bf P}^{*}\in V^{*}
. Then naturally one has
\mu\in V\otimes V
and
\mu^{-1}\in V^{*}\otimes V^{*}

18
18

18

In this section
\mu
stands for a
2\times 2
matrix not the square root of the determinant.
.

The QSC equations are then written

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}\otimes\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{*}=-\,\mu\,{\bf P}^{*}\otimes{\bf P}\,\mu^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}\,,

(66a)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}=\left(1+\frac{1}{F}\,{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}\right)\,\mu\,\left(1+\frac{1}{F}\,{\bf P}^{*}\otimes{\bf P}\right)\,,

(66b)

\displaystyle\Tr{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}=\frac{1}{F}-F\,,

(66c)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{F}\vphantom{F}\,F=1{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}\,.

(66d)

It should be
{(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}=1
if
\epsilon^{ab}\mu_{ab}\neq 0
.

Among consequences of these relations, we recall the most important ones:
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{-}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{-}=\frac{1}{F^{2}}\mu_{-}
,
\det\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{+}=\det\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{+}=\frac{1}{F^{4}}\det\mu_{+}
(and the same property for
\det\mu=\det\mu_{-}+\det\mu_{+}
);

also
\Tr{\left(\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}\otimes\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{*}\,{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}\right)}
is free from branch points.

Square root simplification
We derived a strong no-go theorem about possibility of square root cuts: Assuming that
\mu
have square root type branch points yields non-trivial result only in the case
F^{2}\neq 1
(thus
\mathsf{PSU}
systems are excluded) and moreover
\mu_{ab}\propto\epsilon_{ab}
. This is the case of a Hubbard-type model discussed in Section
6
.

\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{Q}\vphantom{Q}\simeq Q

(A)

We can perform an equivalent analysis using the monodromy bootstrap requirement (
27
) and arrive to a different set of Riemann-Hilbert equations. The procedure is of the same style, we hence delegate technical details to Appendix
A
. Now it is convenient to consider
\mu\in V\otimes V^{*}
, that is
\mu_{a}{}^{b}
in components. For instance one will find
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}=r\,\mu_{a}{}^{b}\,{\bf P}_{b}
and so on. The QSC equations turn out to be the following ones:

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}\otimes\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{*}=\mu\,{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}\,\mu^{-1}\,,

(67a)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}=\left(1+\frac{1}{F}\,{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}\right)\,\mu\,\left(1-{F}\,{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}\right)\,,

(67b)

\displaystyle\Tr{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}=\frac{1}{F}-F\,,

(67c)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{F}\vphantom{F}/F=\det\mu=1\,.

(67d)

We note in particular that
\left(1+\frac{1}{F}\,{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}\right)\left(1-{F}\,{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}\right)=1
and therefore
\Tr\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}=\Tr\mu
which, given periodicity of
\mu
, implies that
\Tr\mu
is a cut-free function. The function
F
has also no cuts anywhere.

Similarly to the case B, if we assume that
{\bf P}_{a},{\bf P}^{a}
have square root branch points then it follows that
\mu_{a}{}^{b}
have square root branch points (or no branch points at all). It is therefore reasonable to attempt square root branch points only for
\mu_{a}{}^{b}
in which case we hit even stronger no-go theorem:

Square root impossibility

If we assume that
\mu
has square root branch points than it automatically implies that
\mu
has no branch points at all.

If
\mu
has no branch points, we can then use (
67b
) to conclude that
\mu
commutes with
{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}
. Then, by (
67a
),
{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}
is free from cuts as well and so there is a gauge choice in which
{\bf P},{\bf P}^{*}
are free from cuts separately. From here, it is an easy exercise to propagate this conclusion to all Q-functions. Hence we recover
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
Q-system with no cuts, this could be for instance a rational spin chain.

\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{Q}\vphantom{Q}\simeq\bar{Q}^{*}
,
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{Q}}\vphantom{\bar{Q}}\simeq Q^{*}

(C)

We can also consider two
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
Q-systems coupled to one another through the analytic continuation. It basically doubles all the equations, and we are strongly motivated by AdS
3
/CFT
2
integrability to include this case, to be discussed in Section
7
.

To formulate the system of equations, we set
{\bf P}\in V,{\bf P}^{*}\in V^{*}
,
\bar{\bf P}\in\bar{V}
,
\bar{\bf P}^{*}\in\bar{V}^{*}
. Then
\mu
and
\bar{\mu}
are gluing functions between
V
and
\bar{V}
:
\mu\in\bar{V}\otimes V
,
\bar{\mu}\in V\otimes\bar{V}
. The equations read

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}\otimes\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{*}=\,-\bar{\mu}\,{\bar{\bf P}}^{*}\otimes\bar{{\bf P}}\,{\bar{\mu}}\vphantom{\mu}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\bar{\mu}}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}\,,\quad\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\bf P}}\vphantom{\bar{\bf P}}\otimes\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\bf P}}\vphantom{\bar{\bf P}}^{*}=-\,\mu\,{\bf P}^{*}\otimes{\bf P}\,\mu^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\bar{\omega}}}}}

(68a)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}=\left(1+\frac{1}{\bar{F}}\,\bar{\bf P}\otimes\bar{\bf P}^{*}\right)\,\mu\,\left(1+\frac{1}{F}\,{\bf P}^{*}\otimes{\bf P}\right)\,,\quad\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\mu}}\vphantom{\bar{\mu}}=\left(1+\frac{1}{F}\,{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}\right)\,\bar{\mu}\,\left(1+\frac{1}{\bar{F}}\,\bar{\bf P}^{*}\otimes\bar{\bf P}\right)\,,

(68b)

\displaystyle\Tr{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}=\frac{1}{F}-F\,,\quad\Tr\bar{\bf P}\otimes\bar{\bf P}^{*}=\frac{1}{\bar{F}}-{\bar{F}}\,,

(68c)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{F}\vphantom{F}\,\bar{F}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\bar{\mu}}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}=F\,\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{F}}\vphantom{\bar{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\bar{\omega}}}}}=1\,.

(68d)

As before
F,\bar{F}
are meromorphic functions of Zhukovsky variable
x
.

\omega,\bar{\omega}
can be computed using relations

\omega^{i{\dot{i}}}=-\bar{\mu}_{a{\dot{a}}}\,(\mathcal{Q}^{a|i}\,\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{{\dot{a}}|{\dot{i}}})^{-}
,
\bar{\omega}^{{\dot{i}}i}=-\mu_{{\dot{a}}a}\,(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{{\dot{a}}|{\dot{i}}}\,\mathcal{Q}^{a|i})^{-}
.
By taking their determinants, we derive
\det\omega/\det\bar{\omega}=\det\mu/\det\bar{\mu}
. From here we apply the logic that precedes (
48
) to conclude
{(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\omega}}+{{\rm p}_{\bar{\mu}}}+{{\rm p}_{\bar{\omega}}}+{{\rm p}_{\mu}}}}=1
. Hence, if we rescale
\bar{{\bf P}}^{*}
and
\bar{F}
to absorb the factor
{(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\bar{\mu}}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}
, the factor
{(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\bar{\omega}}}}}
is also removed from the above equations leaving no sign factors of this type at all.

A curious difference of these equations compared to the single-copy case
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{Q}\vphantom{Q}\simeq Q^{*}
is that now
\mu,\bar{\mu}
cannot be symmetrised/antisymmetrised due to their index structure. Instead, we see that
\mu
and
\bar{\mu}^{\rm T}
satisfy exactly the same Baxter-type equation (
68b
) and it is conceivable that the restriction of the system to a case where a linear combination of
\mu
and
\bar{\mu}^{\rm T}
vanishes is consistent.

Square root and no-cut impossibility
It is
impossible
that
\mu,\bar{\mu}
have branch points of the square root type.

Remarkably, by making system larger we did not add but removed the only possibility to achieve square roots. The basic reason is the following: if in the
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
case equation
{\bf P}^{a}\mu_{ab}{\bf P}^{b}=0
can be saved by assuming that
\mu_{ab}
is antisymmetric, in
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)^{2}
we inevitably arrive to
\bar{\bf P}^{\dot{a}}\mu_{{\dot{a}}b}{\bf P}^{b}=0
which cannot have nontrivial solutions assuming
{\bf P}
and
\bar{\bf P}
are different functions.

\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{Q}\vphantom{Q}\simeq\bar{Q}^{*}
,
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{Q}}\vphantom{\bar{Q}}\simeq Q

(D)

It is natural now to ask what other outer automorphisms can we employ when considering two
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
Q-systems. In total, there are
8=2^{3}
options: the automorphisms
Q\leftrightarrow Q^{*},\bar{Q}\leftrightarrow{\bar{Q}}\vphantom{Q}^{*}
, (
Q\leftrightarrow\bar{Q},Q^{*}\leftrightarrow\bar{Q}\vphantom{Q}^{*}
) generate the dihedral group with eight elements. But only the outlined above case D is the one we yet should consider. Indeed, not involving the swap
Q\leftrightarrow\bar{Q}
leaves the system decoupled, and we can always set
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{Q}\vphantom{Q}\simeq\bar{Q}^{*}
, this is basically the choice of a definition of how to parameterise
\bar{Q}
. Then the only freedom remains: to choose
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{Q}}\vphantom{\bar{Q}}
as
Q
or
Q^{*}
which is, respectively, cases D and C.

To avoid confusion in interpretation, we spell out the explicit crossing equation in case D:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}=\bar{\mu}^{-1}\cdot\omega\cdot\mathcal{Q}\,,\quad\mathcal{Q}=\mu^{-1}\cdot\bar{\omega}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{Q}}\,.

(69)

It is an analog of (
27
) and (
28
), but now
\bar{\mu}\in V\otimes{\bar{V}}
whereas
\mu\in\bar{V}\otimes V^{*}
. We also muted the presence of
{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}
and
{\check{\mathsf{h}}}
to avoid cluttering, but these gauge-transformation functions are generically non-trivial.

The corresponding QSC equations are

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}\otimes\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{*}=-\,\bar{\mu}\,{\bar{\bf P}}^{*}\otimes\bar{{\bf P}}\,{\bar{\mu}}\vphantom{\mu}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\bar{\mu}}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}\,,\quad\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\bf P}}\vphantom{\bar{\bf P}}\otimes\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\bf P}}\vphantom{\bar{\bf P}}^{*}=\,\mu\,{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}\,\mu^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\bar{\omega}}}}}\,,

(70a)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}=\left(1+\frac{1}{\bar{F}}\,\bar{\bf P}\otimes\bar{\bf P}^{*}\right)\,\mu\,\left(1-F\,{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}\right)\,,\quad\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\mu}}\vphantom{\bar{\mu}}=\left(1+\frac{1}{F}\,{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}\right)\,\bar{\mu}\,\left(1+\frac{1}{\bar{F}}\,\bar{\bf P}^{*}\otimes\bar{\bf P}\right)\,,

(70b)

\displaystyle\Tr{\bf P}\otimes{\bf P}^{*}=\frac{1}{F}-F\,,\quad\Tr\bar{\bf P}\otimes\bar{\bf P}^{*}=\frac{1}{\bar{F}}-{\bar{F}}\,,

(70c)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{F}\vphantom{F}\bar{F}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\bar{\mu}}}+{{\rm p}_{\omega}}}}=F/\,\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{F}}\vphantom{\bar{F}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\times(-1)^{{{\rm p}_{\mu}}+{{\rm p}_{\bar{\omega}}}}}=1\,.

(70d)

Since the classifying automorphism of the case D is cyclic of order four, we won’t ask the question about existence of a solution with square-root cuts. An analogous question about fourth-order branch points is of course interesting, but there is no known to us physical model which would motivate its exploration. Overall, case D remains to us a curious possibility, understanding of its significance is a subject for separate study.

6
Quantum spectral curve for Hubbard model

In this section, we shall use the developed formalism to describe spectrum of not only the original Hubbard model but also, and foremost, of its ‘inhomogeneous’ generalisation based on Beisert’s S-matrix
Beisert:2005tm
. The original model is recovered in a limit as will be recalled in subsection
6.3
.

Because (two copies of) inhomogeneous Hubbard model emerge from AdS
5
/CFT
4
spectral problem at large volume, the corresponding QSC can also in principle be derived from AdS
5
/CFT
4
QSC in this approximation. Indeed, Section 5 of
Gromov:2014caa
was an important inspiration for us. Yet, the Hubbard model QSC was not explicitly formulated and studied as a self-contained Q-system before.

There is a novel conceptual aspect which is different from QSC for AdS
5
/CFT
4
: we need to use also gauge transformations as continuous symmetries to successfully close a system of Riemann-Hilbert problems in Hubbard case. The gauge symmetries manifest themselves through the necessarily non-trivial function
F
, the latter is constrained but not fully fixed by the monodromy requirements. It plays the role of the source term in Hubbard QSC, similar to the role of a shifted ratio of Drinfeld polynomial
Q_{\theta}=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{L}(u-\theta_{\ell})
in Wronskian Bethe equations
W(Q_{1},Q_{2})=Q_{\theta}

Pronko:1998xa
;
Mukhin2007BetheAO
;
MTV
;
Chernyak:2020lgw
for
\mathsf{SU}(2)
XXX spin chain
19
19

19

Given this analogy, it would be interesting to consider a quantum algebra based on centrally extended
\mathsf{PSU}(2|2)
and investigate whether
F
, probably together with other functions, can be used to label its finite-dimensional irreps.
.

6.1
Riemann-Hilbert problems for
{{\bf P}\mu}
- and
{\bf Q}\omega
-systems

Let us summarise our findings for
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
QSC of type B under the assumption that the branch points are of square root type. QSC can be formulated in terms of equations for functions
{\bf P}_{a}
,
{\bf P}^{a}
,
{\bf Q}_{i}
,
{\bf Q}^{i}
and
\mu,\omega,F
:

{\bf P}\mu
-system:

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}=\frac{\mu}{F}\epsilon_{ab}{\bf P}^{b}\,,\ \ \overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{a}=-\frac{F}{\mu}\,\epsilon^{ab}\,{\bf P}_{b}\,,\ \ \mu-\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}=\epsilon^{ab}{\bf P}_{a}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{b}\,,\ \ {\bf P}^{a}{\bf P}_{a}=\frac{1}{F}-F\,,

(71)

which self-consistently implies

\displaystyle\frac{\mu}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}}=\frac{F}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{F}\vphantom{F}}=F^{2}\,.

(72)

Here
{\bf P}_{a},{\bf P}^{a}
are functions with only one short cut on the defining sheet of the physical kinematics,
\mu
is
\mathtt{i}
-periodic in the mirror kinematics and
F
is a single-valued function of Zhukovsky variable
x
which satisfies
F(x)F(1/x)=1
.

{\bf Q}\omega
-system:

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf Q}}\vphantom{{\bf Q}}_{i}=-\frac{\omega}{F}\epsilon_{ij}{\bf Q}^{j}\,,\ \ \overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf Q}}\vphantom{{\bf Q}}^{i}=\frac{F}{\omega}\,\epsilon^{ij}\,{\bf Q}_{j}\,,\ \ \overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\omega}\vphantom{\omega}-\omega=\epsilon^{ij}{\bf Q}_{i}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf Q}}\vphantom{{\bf Q}}_{j}\,,\ \ {\bf Q}^{i}{\bf Q}_{i}=\frac{1}{F}-F\,,

(73)

which self-consistently implies

\displaystyle\frac{\omega}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\omega}\vphantom{\omega}}=\frac{F}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{F}\vphantom{F}}=F^{2}\,.

(74)

Here
{\bf Q}_{i},{\bf Q}^{i}
are functions with only one long cut on the defining sheet of the mirror kinematics,
\omega
is
\mathtt{i}
-periodic in the physical kinematics and
F
is the same function as in the
{\bf P}\mu
-system.

One can show that
{\bf P}\mu
-system implies
{\bf Q}\omega
-system and vice versa.

Recall that combination
{\mu}/{F}
appearing in
{\bf P}\mu
-system can be represented as
{\mu}/{F}={f^{[2]}}/{{\bar{f}}^{[-2]}}
, where
f,\bar{f}
satisfy (
35
). We can hide its analytic complexity under the cut by introducing a dressing factor
{\sigma}
which is a function with one short cut on the defining sheet and with the following monodromy property

{\sigma}\,\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\sigma}}\vphantom{{\sigma}}=\frac{\mathtt{f}^{[2]}}{\bar{\mathtt{f}}^{[-2]}}\,.

(75)

Here
\mathtt{f},\bar{\mathtt{f}}
have the same cuts as
f,\bar{f}
,
i.e

U\times\mathtt{f}=f,\bar{U}\times\bar{\mathtt{f}}=\bar{f}
, where
U,\bar{U}
are functions with no branch points, they are introduced for future convenience.

Then
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}=\frac{\mu}{F}\epsilon_{ab}{\bf P}^{b}
can be rewritten as

\frac{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\sigma}}\vphantom{{\sigma}}}=\frac{U^{[2]}}{\bar{U}^{[-2]}}{\sigma}\epsilon_{ab}{\bf P}^{b}\,.

(76)

The r.h.s. has no branch points outside a short cut on the real axis and it follows that
\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{a}={\bf P}_{a}/{\sigma}
is a function defined on a two-sheeted Riemann surface, that is a single-valued function of Zhukovsky variable
x
.

Similarly, write
{\bf Q}_{i}=\frac{\mathtt{f}^{[2]}}{\sigma}\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{i}
. Then from
{\bf Q}_{i}=-{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}Ff^{[2]}\bar{f}^{[-2]}\epsilon_{ij}{\bf Q}^{j}_{\downarrow}
it follows that
\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{i}
cannot have cuts in the lower half-plane (of physical kinematics). Using (
73
) gives
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}_{i}=-\frac{1}{\sigma}\mathtt{f}^{[2]}U^{[2]}\bar{U}^{[-2]}\epsilon_{ij}{\bf Q}^{j}=-\frac{1}{\sigma{\hat{\mathsf{h}}}F\bar{\mathtt{f}}^{[-2]}}{\bf Q}^{\downarrow}_{i}
which means that
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}_{i}
has also only one short cut. Thus
\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{i}
is also a function defined on the two-sheeted Riemann surface.

An equivalent factorisation exists also for upper-indexed functions:
{\bf P}^{a}=\frac{1}{\sigma}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{a}
,
{\bf Q}^{i}=\frac{\sigma}{\mathtt{f}^{[2]}}\hat{\mathbf{q}}^{i}
, with the same
\sigma
.

If we perform gauge transformation (
19
) with
h=\frac{1}{\sigma}
then
{\bf P}
simplifies to
\hat{\mathbf{p}}
, although at price that
{\bf Q}
gets complicated. Likewise, the gauge transformation with
h=\frac{\mathtt{f}^{[2]}}{\sigma}
favours
{\bf Q}
by simplifying them to
\hat{\mathbf{q}}
. In either of the cases, we see that
\sigma
cancels from gauge-invariant combinations
{\bf P}_{a}{\bf P}^{b}
and
{\bf Q}_{i}{\bf Q}^{j}
. We shall not be witnessing infinite ladders of cuts in Bethe equations for some of the gradings. The dressing factor is only needed to get Q-system in a gauge where both
{\bf P}
and
{\bf Q}
have simple analytic structure on their defining sheets (of, respectively, the physical and the mirror kinematics), so the role of
\sigma
is different from the AdS
5
/CFT
4
case.

6.2
A chain with centrally extended
\mathfrak{su}(2|2)
symmetry

Different physical models are specified by choosing
F
. It is straightforward to verify that the following expressions solve
F(x)F(\frac{1}{x})=1
:

\displaystyle{F_{\text{ext}}}=\sqrt{\frac{\mathbb{Q}^{-}}{\mathbb{Q}^{+}}}\frac{B_{(+)}}{B_{(-)}}\,,

\displaystyle F_{\text{pol}}=\pm\prod_{k=1}^{M_{\theta}}\frac{x-\theta_{k}}{x\theta_{k}-1}\,,

\displaystyle{F_{\text{exp}}}(\theta)=e^{\theta(x-\frac{1}{x})}\,,

(77)

where

\displaystyle B_{(\pm)}=\prod_{k=1}^{M_{\theta}}\sqrt{\frac{{\mbox{h}}}{2y^{\mp}_{k}}}(\frac{1}{x}-y^{\mp}_{k})\,,

\displaystyle R_{(\pm)}=\prod_{k=1}^{M_{\theta}}\sqrt{\frac{{\mbox{h}}}{2y^{\mp}_{k}}}(x-y^{\mp}_{k})\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}^{\pm}=(-1)^{M_{\theta}}B_{\pm}R_{\pm}\,,

(78)

and
|y_{k}^{\pm}|>1
,
y_{k}^{+}+\frac{1}{y_{k}^{+}}-y_{k}^{-}-\frac{1}{y_{k}^{-}}=\frac{2\mathtt{i}}{{\mbox{h}}}
;
\theta_{i},\theta\in\mathbb{C}

20
20

20

An interesting generalisation is to set
y^{+}+\frac{1}{y^{+}}-y^{-}-\frac{1}{y^{-}}=\frac{2\mathtt{i}m}{{\mbox{h}}}
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Note also that if pick the mirror kinematics convention
\Im(y_{k}^{\pm})>0
instead of
|y_{k}^{\pm}|>1
, then one can represent
{F_{\text{ext}}}
as a fusion of
F_{\text{pol}}
:
{F_{\text{ext}}}^{2}\propto F_{\text{pol}}(\theta_{k}=y_{k}^{+})F_{\text{pol}}(\theta_{k}=y_{k}^{-})
.
.

In this subsection the choice
F={F_{\text{ext}}}
shall be considered. Its salient feature is that
f
has only a finite number of zeros on the defining sheet
21
21

21

Appearance of square roots
\sqrt{\mathbb{Q}}
is an artefact of normalisation choices. They are resolved in physically relevant combinations.
:

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mathbb{Q}^{-}}\,\mathtt{f}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathbb{Q}^{+}}}\,\bar{\mathtt{f}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathtt{f}

\displaystyle=\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{B^{[2n]}_{(+)}}{B^{[2n]}_{(-)}}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathtt{f}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{B^{[-2n]}_{(+)}}{B^{[-2n]}_{(-)}}\,.

(79)

This also means that
\mathcal{Q}_{12|12}=\mathbb{Q}\,(\mathtt{f}^{+})^{2}
has a finite amount of zeroes on the defining sheet. Furthermore, if we take complex conjugation
\bar{y}^{\pm}_{k}=y^{\mp}_{k}
then
F
is a phase
\overline{F}_{\rm ext}\,{F_{\text{ext}}}=1
.

With this choice of
\mathtt{f}
,
\sigma=\sigma_{\rm BES}
– the main building block of the BES/BHL dressing factor
Beisert:2006ib
;
Beisert:2006ez
. Relation (
75
) is essentially
Volin:2009uv
Janik’s crossing equation
Janik:2006dc
. Also,
\sigma_{\rm BES}/\mathtt{f}^{[2]}
is a function with only one long cut in the mirror kinematics, it is the main building block of the mirror dressing factor
Arutyunov:2009kf
;
Volin:2009tqx
.

Large-
u
asymptotics of
{F_{\text{ext}}}
is power-like. We shall also assume (twisted) power-like asymptotics of all Q-functions to solve QSC equations. Furthermore, we will assume the regularity condition: no poles in Q-functions on the defining sheet of the physical kinematics.

From the experience with rational spin chains and AdS/CFT integrability we expect that the exponents of power-like asymptotics of Q-functions relate directly to quantum numbers. For a twisted supersymmetric Q-system
Kazakov:2015efa
, this relation is

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{a}\sim\mathtt{x}_{a}^{-\mathtt{i}u}u^{-\lambda_{a}}\,,

\displaystyle{\bf Q}_{i}\sim\mathtt{y}_{i}^{\mathtt{i}u}u^{-\nu_{i}}\,,

(80)

when
u\rightarrow\infty
. Here
\mathtt{x}_{a},\mathtt{y}_{i}
are twist factors and
\lambda_{a},\nu_{i}
are
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{2|2}
weights of the physical state
22
22

22

In the presence of the twist,
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{2|2}
symmetry of the physical spectrum is broken to Cartan subalgebra. In the absence or degeneration of the twist, the symmetry is fully or partially restored and then the exponents
\lambda_{a},\nu_{i}
should be replaced with the appropriate shifted weights of the symmetry multiplet
Kazakov:2015efa
.
. To get the twist-independent
F=F_{\rm ext}
, we must impose the unimodularity condition on the twist:
\frac{\mathtt{x}_{1}\mathtt{x}_{2}}{\mathtt{y}_{1}\mathtt{y}_{2}}=1
.

Two bosonic
\mathsf{SU}(2)
Dynkin labels are given by the differences
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2},\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}
. Since
{\bf P}_{a}
has only one short cut on the main sheet, it must have trivial monodromy around a circle at infinity so
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
and hence
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}
must be integer
23
23

23

Integrality of
\lambda_{a}
can be relaxed depending on a gauge choice, it is only the invariant combinations
{\bf P}_{a}{\bf P}^{b}
that must always have the stated property. This is enough to insure that
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}
is integer.
. To investigate
\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}
, we look at
\frac{{\bf Q}_{1}}{{\bf Q}_{2}}
which naively has a tower of cuts in the lower half-plane of the physical kinematics. However
\frac{{\bf Q}_{1}}{{\bf Q}_{2}}=\frac{\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1}}{\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2}}
, so the ratio has only one short cut on the real axis. Therefore
\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}
is also an integer and hence the type-B quantum spectral curve with branch points of square root type is compatible with the compact real form
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
.

With
F=F_{\rm ext}
and the above-stated assumptions on Q-functions, the further analysis follows almost word by word section 5 of
Gromov:2014caa
. For completeness we provide the bare minimum of details to reach the exact Bethe equations of the system. Using the ansatz
{\bf P}_{a}\propto{\sigma_{\text{BES}}}\,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{a},{\bf Q}_{i}\propto\frac{\mathtt{f}^{[2]}}{{\sigma_{\text{BES}}}}\,\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{i}
and allowing for twisted asymptotics, we can write

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{a}=\mathtt{x}_{a}^{-\mathtt{i}u}\times\mathbf{p}_{a}(x)\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{i}=\mathtt{y}_{i}^{\mathtt{i}u}\times\frac{\mathbf{q}_{i}(x)}{B_{(-)}}\,.

(81)

The
{\bf P}\mu
and
{\bf Q}\omega
-systems imply
\mathcal{Q}_{a|12}\propto\frac{\mathtt{f}\mathtt{f}^{[2]}}{{\sigma_{\text{BES}}}}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}_{a}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{12|i}\propto{\sigma_{\text{BES}}}B_{(+)}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}_{i}\mathtt{f}^{[2]}
. From the regularity condition it follows that
\mathbf{p}_{a}(x)
and
\mathbf{q}_{i}(x)
are regular on both sheets. Furthermore, compatibility with QQ-relations forces
\mathtt{x}_{1}\mathtt{x}_{2}=1,\mathtt{y}_{1}\mathtt{y}_{2}=1
which shows that the monodromy bootstrap does not allow for an arbitrary twist.

From regularity,
\mathbf{p}_{1}
should be a Zhukovsky polynomial and can then be parameterised as

\mathbf{p}_{1}\propto\prod_{k=1}^{M_{1|\emptyset}}(x-x^{(k)}_{1|\emptyset})\prod_{k=1}^{M_{1|12}}(\frac{1}{x}-x^{(k)}_{1|12})\,,

(82)

where we have split the zeros between the first and second sheet. Similar parameterisations can be introduced for
\mathbf{p}_{2}
and
\mathbf{q}_{i}
but we won’t use them explicitly.

To find
\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}
recall that
\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}
is UHPA while
\mathcal{Q}_{a}|{}^{i}=\mathcal{Q}_{a|j}(\omega^{ji})^{+}

is LHPA. Due to the anti-symmetry,
\omega^{ij}
has only one analytically non-trivial component as an overall prefactor. Then the ratio
\frac{\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}}{\mathcal{Q}_{b|j}}
has this prefactor cancelled out, and now it is easy to see that it cannot have any cuts. Thus
\frac{\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}}{\mathcal{Q}_{b|j}}
must be, up to a twist, a rational function of
u
. Parameterise

\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}\propto\left(\frac{\mathtt{x}_{a}}{\mathtt{y}_{i}}\right)^{-\mathtt{i}u}\mathbb{Q}_{a|i}\times\ell^{+}\,.

(83)

From the QQ-relation
\mathcal{Q}_{1|1}\mathcal{Q}_{2|2}-\mathcal{Q}_{1|2}\mathcal{Q}_{2|1}=\mathcal{Q}_{12|12}
it follows that
\ell=\mathtt{f}
. Then
\mathbb{Q}_{a|i}
is a polynomial in
u
by the regularity assumption.

Bethe equations follow from QQ-relations after shifting and evaluation at zeros of an appropriate Q-function. Here we will consider the following set of nested Bethe equations

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathcal{Q}_{1|1}^{+}}{\mathcal{Q}_{1|1}^{-}}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\mathcal{Q}_{1|\emptyset}=0}=1\,,\quad\left(\frac{\mathcal{Q}_{1|1}^{[2]}}{\mathcal{Q}_{1|1}^{[-2]}}\frac{\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{1|\emptyset}\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{1|12}}{\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{1|\emptyset}\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{1|12}}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\mathcal{Q}_{1|1}=0}=-1\,,\quad\left(\frac{\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{12|12}\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{1|1}}{\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{12|12}\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{1|1}}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\mathcal{Q}_{1|12}=0}=1\,.

(84)

The first equation written out explicitly is

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{1|1}}{\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{1|1}}\bigg\rvert_{\mathcal{Q}_{1|\emptyset}=0}=1\implies\frac{\mathtt{x}_{1}}{\mathtt{y}_{1}}\prod_{k=1}^{M_{\theta}}\sqrt{\frac{y^{-}_{k}}{y^{+}_{k}}}\frac{y^{+}_{k}-\frac{1}{x^{(i)}_{1|\emptyset}}}{y^{-}_{k}-\frac{1}{x^{(i)}_{1|\emptyset}}}\prod_{k=1}^{M_{1|1}}\frac{u^{(i)}_{1|\emptyset}-u^{(k)}_{1|1}+\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2}}{u^{(i)}_{1|\emptyset}-u^{(k)}_{1|1}-\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2}}=1\,.

(85)

For the middle node Bethe equations, all factors of
\mathtt{f}
cancel and only polynomials in
u
remain, the Bethe equations become

\frac{1}{\mathtt{y}^{2}_{1}}\prod_{k=1}^{M_{1|1}}\frac{u^{(i)}_{1|1}-u^{(k)}_{1|1}+\mathtt{i}}{u^{(i)}_{1|1}-u^{(k)}_{1|1}-\mathtt{i}}\prod_{k=1}^{M_{1|\emptyset}}\frac{u^{(i)}_{1|1}-u^{(k)}_{1|\emptyset}-\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2}}{u^{(i)}_{1|1}-u^{(k)}_{1|\emptyset}+\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2}}\prod^{M_{1|12}}_{k=1}\frac{u^{(i)}_{1|1}-u^{(k)}_{1|12}-\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2}}{u^{(i)}_{1|1}-u^{(k)}_{1|12}+\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2}}=-1\,.

(86)

The last equation is almost identical to (
85
):

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{Q}_{12|12}^{-}}{\mathcal{Q}_{12|12}^{+}}\frac{\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{1|1}}{\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{1|1}}\bigg\rvert_{\mathcal{Q}_{1|12}=0}=1\implies\frac{\mathtt{x}_{1}}{\mathtt{y}_{1}}\prod_{k=1}^{M_{\theta}}\sqrt{\frac{y^{-}_{k}}{y^{+}_{k}}}\frac{x^{(i)}_{1|12}-y^{+}_{k}}{x^{(i)}_{1|12}-y^{-}_{k}}\prod_{k=1}^{M_{1|1}}\frac{u^{(i)}_{1|12}-u^{(k)}_{1|1}+\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2}}{u^{(i)}_{1|12}-u^{(k)}_{1|1}-\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2}}=1\,.

(87)

These equations are well known as part of the asymptotic Bethe equations for AdS
5
/CFT
4

Beisert:2005fw
. In their own right, they describe a spin chain with centrally extended
\mathfrak{su}(2|2)
symmetry
Beisert:2006qh
(a.k.a. inhomogeneous Hubbard model).

6.3
Lieb-Wu equations

To study the original ‘homogeneous’ Hubbard model we can take
F=\frac{1}{x^{M_{\theta}}}=F_{\text{pol}}(\theta_{i}\to\infty)
. The above derivation goes through with minor modifications. Instead of repeating it, we use the procedure from
Beisert:2006qh
: Introduce the parameterisation

x^{(i)}_{1|\emptyset}=\mathtt{i}e^{-\mathtt{i}k_{i}}\,,\quad x^{(i)}_{1|12}=\frac{e^{\mathtt{i}k_{i}}}{\mathtt{i}}\,,\quad u^{(i)}_{1|\emptyset}={\mbox{h}}\sin{k_{i}}\,,\quad u^{(i)}_{1|12}={\mbox{h}}\sin k_{i}\,,\quad\lambda_{i}=\frac{1}{{\mbox{h}}}u^{(i)}_{1|1}\,;

(88)

Fix the twist
\mathtt{y}_{1}=\mathtt{y}_{2}=1,\mathtt{x}_{1}=\frac{1}{\mathtt{x}_{2}}=\prod_{k=1}^{M_{\theta}}\mathtt{i}
and take the limit
y^{+}_{k}\sim\frac{1}{\epsilon},y^{-}_{k}\sim\epsilon,\epsilon\rightarrow 0
to obtain

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{M}\frac{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+\frac{\mathtt{i}}{{\mbox{h}}}}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}-\frac{\mathtt{i}}{{\mbox{h}}}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{i}-\sin{k_{j}}-\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2{\mbox{h}}}}{\lambda_{i}-\sin{k_{j}}+\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2{\mbox{h}}}}=-1\,,\quad i=1,\dots,M\,,

(89a)

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{M}\frac{\sin{k_{i}}-\lambda_{j}+\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2{\mbox{h}}}}{\sin{k_{i}}-\lambda_{j}-\frac{\mathtt{i}}{2{\mbox{h}}}}=e^{\mathtt{i}M_{\theta}k_{i}}\,.\quad i=1,\dots,N\,,

(89b)

where
M=M_{1|1}
and
N=M_{1|\emptyset}+M_{1|12}
. These are exactly Lieb-Wu equations
LiebWu
describing the spectrum of homogeneous Hubbard model. Comparing with equations
(3.95)-(3.96)
from
HubbardBook
shows that
\mathbf{u}=\frac{1}{2\,{\mbox{h}}}
is the coupling constant appearing in the Hamiltonian

H_{\text{Hubbard}}=-\sum_{j=1}^{M_{\theta}}\sum_{a=\uparrow,\downarrow}(c^{\dagger}_{j,a}c_{j+1,a}+c^{\dagger}_{j+1,a}c_{j,a})+\mathbf{u}\sum_{j=1}^{M_{\theta}}(1-2n_{j,\uparrow})(1-2n_{j,\downarrow})\,.

(90)

We refer to
HubbardBook
for an in-depth treatment of the model and explanation of the notation used in (
90
).

The limit
\epsilon\to 0
gives the source term
F=\sqrt{\frac{\mathbb{Q}^{-}}{\mathbb{Q}^{+}}}\frac{B_{(+)}}{B_{(-)}}=\sqrt{\frac{R_{-}}{B_{-}}\frac{B_{+}}{R_{+}}}\rightarrow\frac{1}{x^{M_{\theta}}}
. Of course the choice to send
y^{+},\frac{1}{y^{-}}
to infinity was arbitrary, we could equally well consider
\frac{1}{y^{+}_{k}},y^{-}_{k}\rightarrow\infty
which would lead to
F=x^{M_{\theta}}
. We remark that the limit is non-trivial. For one thing, it violates
|y^{-}_{k}|>1
requiring continuing the inhomogeneities under the cut and changing the reality property of
{F_{\text{ext}}}
to
\overline{{F_{\text{ext}}}}={F_{\text{ext}}}
. For another, the centrally extended symmetry undergoes a type of contraction, only the bosonic subalgebra survives manifestly in the limit
deLeeuw:2015ula
.

There exist other interesting choices of
y^{+}
and
y^{-}
apart from the homogeneous Hubbard limit. In particular,
Frolov:2011wg
studied the cases
y^{+}y^{-}=-1
and
\frac{y^{+}}{y^{-}}=-1
which are Hermitian parity-invariant models. It would be interesting to investigate the thermodynamic limit of these models using QSC.

6.4
T- and Y-systems

Among suggested source terms (
77
), we still have to consider
{F_{\text{exp}}}(\theta)
. It exhibits non-polynomial behaviour natural to the case of thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz (TBA).

TBA for Hubbard model was first developed in the work of Takahashi
Takahashi
. Later on, this approach became a part of technology in derivations of mirror TBA equations and Y-/T-systems of AdS
5
/CFT
4
integrability
Gromov:2009tv
,
Bombardelli:2009ns
;
Gromov:2009bc
;
Arutyunov:2009ur
. These equations, by a meticulous analysis of the discontinuity properties
Cavaglia:2010nm
,
Gromov:2011cx
of Y- and T-functions superposed on the Wronskian solution
Gromov:2010km
of T-functions in terms of Q-functions, were eventually reduced to AdS
5
/CFT
4
QSC
Gromov:2013pga
;
Gromov:2014caa
.

One of the original motivations that launched our work was to circumvent this laboured approach to derive QSC’s. We presumably succeeded for the example of Hubbard model, yet the question remains whether the derived QSC by the monodromy bootstrap can be also derived
via
the TBA route. Fortunately, we do not need to repeat the full TBA computation as most of the work had been carried out in
Cavaglia:2015nta
by Cavaglià, Cornagliotto, Mattelliano, and Tateo. In fact, the discontinuity relations equivalent to (
71
) are already present in that paper, we just need to make a proper decoding of functions (which is done at the end of this section). What was not done in
Cavaglia:2015nta
is connecting analytic properties of the full
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{2|2}
Q-system and of the corresponding T-system, and we shall focus mainly on this task.

Relation between Q- and T-functions is purely algebraic and is valid independently of whether we discuss TBA or not. Hence, our general discussion will be done without any assumption on function
F
. At the end of the section, we return to the concrete choices of
F
, and in particular explicitly relate
{F_{\text{exp}}}(\theta)
to Hubbard model at finite temperature.

\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
T-functions are functions
T_{a,s}
defined on the
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)

\mathbb{L}
-hook,
\{(a,s)|\,s\in[0,2],a\geq 0\}\cup\{(a,s)|\,a\in[0,2],s\geq 0\}
. They satisfy the T-system:

\displaystyle T^{+}_{a,s}T^{-}_{a,s}=T_{a,s+1}T_{a,s-1}+T_{a+1,s}T_{a-1,s}\,.

(91)

Like Q-functions, T-functions shall also have branch points, and then one needs to specify whether (
91
) is valid in the physical or the mirror kinematics. To distinguish the two cases we use the notation
\check{T}_{a,s}
for T-functions of the mirror T-system, and
\hat{T}_{a,s}
for T-functions of the physical T-system. No check or hat over
T_{a,s}
shall refer to general discussion. There is no guarantee that
\check{T}
-system and
\hat{T}
-system are related by a direct analytic continuation precisely due to the recurring stumbling block of the paper: equation (
91
) is non-local. Nevertheless, we shall see that certain
\check{T}
- and
\hat{T}
-functions are indeed related by the continuation, somewhat surprisingly and due to the specific nature of Riemann-Hilbert problems (
71
).

Given a solution to the T-system one can construct another one using gauge transformations

\displaystyle T_{a,s}\rightarrow g_{(++)}^{[a+s]}g_{(+-)}^{[a-s]}g_{(-+)}^{[-a+s]}g_{(--)}^{[-a-s]}T_{a,s}\,,

(92)

implying that not
T_{a,s}
but rather their invariant combinations

Y_{a,s}=\frac{T_{a,s-1}T_{a,s+1}}{T_{a-1,s}T_{a+1,s}}

(93)

encode physical properties. Just as we distinguish physical and mirror T-functions, we should distinguish
\hat{Y}_{a,s}
and
\check{Y}_{a,s}
.

There exist a well-established procedure to generate a solution of the T-system using Q-functions
Tsuboi:2009ud
. In the specific case of
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
it reads

\displaystyle T_{0,s\geq 0}=(-1)^{s}Q^{[s]}_{\emptyset|\emptyset}Q^{[-s]}_{12|12}\,,

\displaystyle T_{1,s\geq 1}=(-1)^{s+1}\epsilon^{ab}Q^{[s]}_{a|\emptyset}Q^{[-s]}_{b|12}\,,

\displaystyle T_{2,s\geq 2}=(-1)^{s}Q^{[s]}_{12|\emptyset}Q_{\emptyset|12}^{[-s]}\,,

(94a)

\displaystyle T_{a\geq 0,0}=(-1)^{a}Q_{12|12}^{[a]}Q_{\emptyset|\emptyset}^{[-a]}\,,

\displaystyle T_{a\geq 1,1}=-\epsilon^{ij}Q^{[a]}_{12|i}Q^{[-a]}_{\emptyset|j}\,,

\displaystyle T_{a\geq 2,2}=(-1)^{a}Q^{[a]}_{12|\emptyset}Q^{[-a]}_{\emptyset|12}\,.

(94b)

The signs are picked following
Kazakov:2015efa
. As a default, we evaluate T-functions on the real axis. Hence, assuming UHPA Q-functions, it is important to specify kinematics when evaluating
Q^{[-s]}_{A|I}
: evaluating them in the physical kinematics yields
\hat{T}_{a,s}
while the mirror kinematics evaluation yields
\check{T}_{a,s}
.

Gauge transformations of the Q-system generate two gauge transformations of the T-system:

\displaystyle Q_{A|I}\rightarrow g_{(+)}^{[|A|-|I|]}g_{(-)}^{[-|A|+|I|]}Q_{A|I}\,,

\displaystyle T_{a,s}\rightarrow g_{(+)}^{[a+s]}g_{(+)}^{[-a-s]}g_{(-)}^{[s-a]}g_{(-)}^{[-s+a]}T_{a,s}\,.

(95)

Wronskian solution (
94
) is not invariant under Hodge transformation of the underlying Q-system but the transformation rule is not difficult: switching the Q-system with its Hodge dual is equivalent, up to overall signs, to sending all shifts in (
94
) to their negative values. We will denote the T-functions obtained using Hodge duality by
T^{*}_{a,s}

Using Wronskian solution (
94
), we can deduce the analytic properties of the T- and Y-system from Q-functions. There does not exist a global gauge in which all T-functions have the simplest possible analytic properties. It is then advantageous to focus either on the right (
s\geq a
) or the upper (
a\geq s
) band of the
\mathbb{L}
-hook.

Consider first the right band. From the Q-system we know that
\mathcal{Q}_{a|\emptyset}\propto\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{a},\mathcal{Q}_{a|12}\propto\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}_{a}
, where all omitted factors are independent of the index of the Q-functions. It follows that it is possible to do a gauge transformation such that

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{0,s\geq 0}=1\,,

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{1,s\geq 1}=\epsilon^{ab}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{[s]}_{a}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}_{b}^{[-s]}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{2,s\geq 2}=(\epsilon^{ab}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{+}_{a}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{-}_{b})^{[s]}(\epsilon^{cd}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}^{+}_{c}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}^{-}_{d})^{[-s]}\,,

(96a)

\displaystyle\check{T}_{0,s\geq 0}=1\,,

\displaystyle\check{T}_{1,s\geq 1}=\epsilon^{ab}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{[s]}_{a}\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{b}^{[-s]}\,,

\displaystyle\check{T}_{2,s\geq 2}=\check{T}_{1,1}^{[s]}\check{T}_{1,1}^{[-s]}\,.

(96b)

This gauge transformation is not of Wronskian type (
95
) because no such transformation can set
T_{0,s\geq 0}=1
. Since
\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{a}
is a single-valued function of Zhukovsky variable,
T_{1,s\geq 1}
has only two cuts. It’s interesting to notice that the four different sheets of
T_{1,s\geq 1}
can be interpreted as a choice of the kinematics and Hodge duality:

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{1,s}=\epsilon^{ab}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{[s]}_{a}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}^{[-s]}_{b}\,,

\displaystyle\check{T}_{1,s}=\epsilon^{ab}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{[s]}_{a}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{[-s]}_{b}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{T}^{*}_{1,s}=\epsilon^{ab}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}^{[s]}_{a}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{[-s]}_{b}\,,

\displaystyle\check{T}^{*}_{1,s}=\epsilon^{ab}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}^{[s]}_{a}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{p}}}^{[-s]}_{b}\,.

(97a)

Hence deciding what is the right kinematics is to a large extent artificial (although it is of course necessary to make the discussion concrete).

Turning to the upper-band
a\geq s
, it is worth remembering that there is a gauge where
{\bf Q}_{i}
become
{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}_{i}
- single-valued functions of Zhukovsky variable. We can then simply repeat all the above argumentation for the upper band using
{\bf Q}_{i}
instead of
{\bf P}_{a}
. It is hence more informative to keep using the same gauge as for the right band and check what are the upper-band T-functions:

\displaystyle T_{a\geq 0,0}=(-1)^{a}\left(\frac{U^{[2]}}{\bar{U}^{[-2]}}\frac{1}{F}\right)^{[a]_{D}}\,,

\displaystyle T_{a\geq 1,1}=\left(\frac{U^{+}}{\bar{U}^{-}}\right)^{[a]_{D}}\left(\frac{1}{F\bar{U}^{[-2]}U^{[2]}}\right)^{[-a]}\epsilon^{ij}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}^{[a]}_{i}\hat{\mathbf{q}}^{[-a]}_{j}\,.

(98)

The negative shifts are considered in the physical kinematics to obtain physical T-functions, and in the mirror kinematics to get
\check{T}_{a,s}
. We also use the following notation:
f^{[a]_{D}}=f^{[a-1]}f^{[a-3]}\dots f^{[-a+1]}
.

We discuss now properties of
Y_{1,1},Y_{2,2}
. Recall the explicit expressions in terms of T-functions

\displaystyle Y_{1,1}=\frac{T_{1,0}T_{1,2}}{T_{0,1}T_{2,1}}\,,

\displaystyle Y_{2,2}=\frac{T_{2,1}T_{2,3}}{T_{1,2}T_{3,2}}\,.

(99)

Using the gauge of the Wronskian solution (
94
), we have
T_{2,3}=T_{3,2}
and so the product of
Y_{1,1}
and
Y_{2,2}
is given by

Y_{1,1}Y_{2,2}=\frac{\hat{T}_{1,0}}{\hat{T}_{0,1}}=\frac{\check{T}_{1,0}}{\check{T}_{0,1}}=\frac{Q_{12|12}^{+}}{Q_{12|12}^{-}}=\frac{(f^{[2]})^{2}}{f^{2}}=\frac{1}{F^{2}}\,,

(100)

where all expressions are evaluated slightly above the real axis.

Clearly
Y_{1,1}Y_{2,2}
have a cut on the real axis and furthermore, using the Wronskian parameterisation, we can deduce that this cut is present in both
Y_{1,1}
and
Y_{2,2}
. To find the analytic continuation around this cut we use that
Y_{2,2}=\frac{T_{2,1}}{T_{1,2}}
in the Wronskian gauge and substitute in the explicit form of Q-functions:
\mathcal{Q}_{a|12}=-\bar{U}^{[-2]}U\frac{\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{a}}{\sigma}\mathtt{f}\mathtt{f}^{[2]},\mathcal{Q}_{\emptyset|i}=\mathtt{f}^{[2]}\frac{\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{i}}{\sigma}
. Then the analytic continuation around this cut is computed as

\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{Y}\vphantom{Y}_{2,2}=F^{2}Y_{2,2}=\frac{1}{Y_{1,1}}\,.

(101)

Let us now comment on reality. To constrain to real solutions, we shall demand that complex conjugation of Q-functions is a symmetry of the Q-system and can then include a combination of a gauge transformation, H-rotation, and possibly Hodge duality. Since complex conjugation changes the positive shifts to negative we must also include extra signs to preserve QQ-relations. Explicitly

\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{A|I}=(-1)^{\frac{(|A|+|I|)(|A|+|I|-1)}{2}}S\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}\,,

(102)

where
S
is a symmetry transformation.

As usual, working either with or without Hodge duality gives two distinct options. Moreover, we have to decide in which kinematics we demand the reality property, which
a priory
gives us four options in total to consider. We shall pick one case for now and focus on the physical kinematics and no Hodge duality:

\displaystyle\overline{{\bf P}}_{a}=h\,(H_{b})_{a}{}^{b}{\bf P}_{b}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{{\bf Q}}_{i}=\frac{1}{h\,\omega}\,(H_{f}^{\prime})_{i}{}^{j}{\bf Q}_{j}=-\frac{1}{h\,F}\,(H_{f}^{\prime})_{i}{}^{k}\epsilon_{kj}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf Q}}\vphantom{{\bf Q}}^{j}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{F}=\frac{1}{F}\,,

(103)

where the fermionic H-rotation is performed by the matrix
H_{f}
with coefficients
(H_{f})_{i}{}^{j}=\frac{1}{\omega}(H_{f}^{\prime})_{i}{}^{j}
, we pulled out the factor
1/\omega
for future convenience. To derive the last relation use
\mathcal{Q}_{12|12}=\frac{1}{g^{2}}
to find
\frac{g^{2}}{\bar{g}^{2}}=\det({H^{\prime}_{f}}^{+})\det(H_{b}^{+})/(\omega^{+})^{2}
. Periodicity of
H^{\prime}_{f},H_{b}
and
\omega
in the physical kinematics results in
\frac{\overline{g}^{+}}{\overline{g}^{-}}=\frac{g^{+}}{g^{-}}
so that
\overline{F}=\frac{1}{F}
.

Let us find the analytic properties of
H_{b}
and
H_{f}^{\prime}
. Following the steps detailed in footnote
12
with
\overline{{\bf P}}_{a}
playing the role of
{\bf P}^{\downarrow}_{a}
we find that it is always possible to reparametrise
h
and
H_{b}
so that
h
has only one short cut on the real axis and
H_{b}
has no branch points. Recall then that
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf Q}}\vphantom{{\bf Q}}^{i}
is analytic in the lower half-plane. Because
\bar{{\bf Q}}_{i}
is analytic there as well, we use periodicity of
H_{f}^{\prime}
to derive that
H_{f}^{\prime}
cannot have any cuts. In summary both
H_{b}
and
H_{f}^{\prime}
are periodic functions without cuts.

Now we turn to the analytic structure of
h
. It is useful to write down the relations that follow from compatibility with
\bar{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}}=\mathcal{Q}

24
24

24

To derive the relation for
\bar{H}_{b}H_{b}
we use that
\bar{H}_{b}H_{b}{\bf P}^{[2n]}=\frac{1}{(\bar{h}h)^{[2n]}}{\bf P}^{[2n]}
and assume that
{\bf P}
and
{\bf P}^{[2]}
are linearly-independent vectors. Since
\bar{H}_{b}H_{b}
is a
2\times 2
matrix it has 2 eigenvalues. If the eigenvalue is degenerate then
(h\bar{h})^{[2]}=h\bar{h}
and
h\bar{h}\bar{H}_{b}H_{b}=1
as claimed. If there are two distinct eigenvalues then use also
\bar{H}_{b}H_{b}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}=\frac{1}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{h\bar{h}}\vphantom{h\bar{h}}}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}
. It must be that either
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{h\bar{h}}\vphantom{h\bar{h}}=h\bar{h}
or
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{h\bar{h}}\vphantom{h\bar{h}}=(h\bar{h})^{[2]}
. If
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{h\bar{h}}\vphantom{h\bar{h}}=h\bar{h}
then
{\bf P}\epsilon\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}=0
but this is a contradiction because
{\bf P}\epsilon\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}\propto\frac{1}{F}-F\neq 0
; if
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{h\bar{h}}\vphantom{h\bar{h}}=(h\bar{h})^{[2]}
then
h\bar{h}=(h\bar{h})^{[2]}
because
h,\bar{h}
only have cuts on the real axis. We conclude that
h\bar{h}\bar{H}_{b}H=1
, the same argumentation also gives
\frac{1}{h\bar{h}}\bar{H}_{f}H_{f}=1
.
:

\displaystyle\bar{h}\,h\,\bar{H}_{b}\,H_{b}=1\,,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\bar{h}\,h}\frac{1}{\bar{\omega}\,\omega}\bar{H}^{\prime}_{f}\,H^{\prime}_{f}=1\,.

(104)

Multiplying these two consistency conditions results in
\omega\,\bar{\omega}=H_{b}\,\bar{H}_{b}\,H^{\prime}_{f}\,\bar{H}^{\prime}_{f}
and thus
\omega\,\bar{\omega}
has no cuts. Analytic continuation of
h
through its single cut follows from the
{\bf P}\mu
-system:
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathcal{Q}}\vphantom{\mathcal{Q}}_{a|\emptyset}=-\frac{1}{\omega}\mathcal{Q}_{a|12}
. We take the complex conjugation of this equation and use that the analytic continuation commutes with complex conjugation. Since
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{a|12}=-\frac{1}{h\omega^{2}}\det H^{\prime}_{f}\,(H_{b})_{a}{}^{b}\mathcal{Q}_{b|12}
, we find

\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{h}\vphantom{h}h=-\frac{1}{\omega\bar{\omega}}\det H^{\prime}_{f}\,.

(105)

We conclude that
h
is a single-valued function of Zhukovsky variable and that
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{h}\vphantom{h}h
is a periodic function.

Perform now a symmetry transformation

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{a}\rightarrow\bar{h}_{A}\bar{P}^{A}_{b}(\bar{A}_{b})_{a}{}^{b}{\bf P}_{b}\,,

\displaystyle{\bf Q}_{i}\rightarrow\frac{1}{\bar{h}_{A}}\bar{P}^{A}_{f}(\bar{A}_{f})_{i}{}^{j}{\bf Q}_{j}\,,

(106)

with
A_{b/f}

\mathtt{i}
-periodic matrices with unit determinant,
P^{A}_{b/f}
periodic functions without cuts and
h_{A}
a single-valued function of Zhukovsky variable. Our goal is to simplify
h\,H_{b},\,\frac{1}{h}\,H^{\prime}_{f}
. It will be convenient to factorise expressions:
h\,H_{b}=h\,\sqrt{\det H_{b}}\times\frac{H_{b}}{\sqrt{\det H_{b}}}
,
\frac{1}{h}\,H_{f}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{h}\,\sqrt{\det H_{f}^{\prime}}\times\frac{H_{f}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\det H_{f}^{\prime}}}
, and furthermore we shall define
M_{b}=\mathtt{i}\,\frac{H_{b}\,\epsilon}{\sqrt{\det H_{b}}}
,
M_{f}=\mathtt{i}\,\frac{H_{f}^{\prime}\epsilon}{\sqrt{\det H_{f}^{\prime}}}
.

Matrices
M_{b},M_{f}
are Hermitian due to consistency conditions (
104
) and they change under transformation (
106
) following the rule

\displaystyle M_{b}\rightarrow A_{b}M_{b}A_{b}^{\dagger}\,,

\displaystyle M_{f}\rightarrow A_{f}M_{f}A_{f}^{\dagger}\,.

(107)

Use the standard trick
M_{b/f}=x_{\mu}\sigma^{\mu},\sigma^{\mu}=(\mathds{1}_{2\times 2},\sigma^{i=1,2,3})
to identify
M_{b/f}
with the 4-vector
x_{\mu}
. Recall that the action (
107
) in the case
A\in\mathsf{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
is equivalent to Lorentz transformations of
x_{\mu}
. Since
\det M_{b/f}=-1
the 4-vector
x_{\mu}
is space-like and we can bring it to any Pauli-matrix using
A_{b/f}
.

We are left with prefactors, we parametrise these as
h\sqrt{\det H_{b}}=\mathtt{i}\sqrt{\frac{h}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{h}\vphantom{h}}}P_{b},\,\frac{1}{h}\sqrt{\det H^{\prime}_{f}}=\mathtt{i}\sqrt{\frac{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{h}\vphantom{h}}{h}}P_{f}
with
P_{b/f}
periodic functions. We consider first the non-periodic factor
\sqrt{\frac{h}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{h}\vphantom{h}}}
, under (
106
) we find
\sqrt{\frac{h}{\tilde{h}}}\rightarrow\frac{h_{A}}{\bar{h}_{A}}\sqrt{\frac{h}{\tilde{h}}}
. By taking
h_{A}=(\frac{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{h}\vphantom{h}}{h})^{\frac{1}{4}}
we set this factor to
1
since
\bar{h}_{A}h_{A}=1
. Finally, the periodic factors transform as

\displaystyle P_{b}\rightarrow\frac{P^{A}_{b}}{\bar{P}^{A}_{b}}\,P_{b}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\omega}P_{f}\rightarrow\frac{P^{A}_{f}\bar{P}^{A}_{f}}{\omega^{\prime}}\,P_{f}\,.

(108)

Here we have used that
\omega\rightarrow\omega^{\prime}=(\bar{P}^{A}_{f})^{2}\omega
. By picking
P_{b}^{A}
and
P_{f}^{A}
appropriately we can send
P_{b}\rightarrow 1,P_{f}\rightarrow 1
. This is possible since
P_{b}\bar{P}_{b}=1,\bar{P}_{f}=P_{f}
which can be verified using the explicit expressions
P_{b}=\frac{\sqrt{\det H_{b}}\sqrt{\det H^{\prime}_{f}}}{\sqrt{\omega\bar{\omega}}},P_{f}=\sqrt{\omega\bar{\omega}}
as follows from (
105
)
25
25

25

The sign choice when taking the square root of (
105
) is irrelevant because it can be reabsorbed by an
A_{b/f}
rotation.
.

In conclusion we can pick
hH_{b}=\frac{1}{h}H^{\prime}_{f}=(\vec{n}\,\cdot\vec{\sigma})\epsilon
with
\vec{n}
a unit vector. It follows that
\bar{\omega}\omega=1,\bar{\mu}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}
and
\check{T}_{a,s}
, in gauge (
96b
), are manifestly real, and so
\check{Y}_{a,s}
are real. A choice of
\vec{n}
amounts to picking a specific basis of Q-functions. There exists a preferred basis in physical systems singled out by asymptotic conditions, in those cases there also exists a natural choice of
\vec{n}
.

One of the natural choices is
\vec{n}\vec{\sigma}=\sigma^{3}
which results in

\displaystyle\overline{{\bf P}}_{a}=|\epsilon_{ab}|{\bf P}_{b}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{{\bf Q}}_{i}=\frac{|\epsilon_{ij}|}{\omega}{\bf Q}_{j}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{F}=\frac{1}{F}\,,

\displaystyle\text{(physical kinematics)}\,,

(109a)

\displaystyle\overline{{\bf P}}_{a}=\frac{\mu}{F}(-1)^{a}{\bf P}^{a}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{{\bf Q}}_{i}=F(-1)^{i+1}{\bf Q}^{i}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{F}=F

\displaystyle\text{(mirror kinematics)}\,,

(109b)

where
|\epsilon_{ab}|=\delta_{a1}\delta_{b2}+\delta_{a2}\delta_{b1}
.

All reasoning above was performed assuming the physical kinematics and (
109a
) is the final outcome. Then we used
{\bf P}\mu
- and
{\bf Q}\omega
-relations to derive (
109b
) which is clearly a reality demand (
102
) with Hodge duality and in the mirror kinematics. Moreover, assuming the most general reality demand (
102
) with Hodge duality and the mirror kinematics, we can simplify it to (
109b
), and then use
{\bf P}\mu
- and
{\bf Q}\omega
-equations to get to (
109a
) as well. Hence changing the kinematics in (
102
) is equivalent to the change of the choice of whether to take Hodge dual. So there are only two different reality demands, not four which was our original expectation.

The analysis of the Hodge dual case in the physical kinematics (or of the no-Hodge dual case in the mirror kinematics) is analogous. The reality condition, in a basis similar to the one in (
109
), simplifies to

\displaystyle\overline{{\bf P}}_{a}=(-1)^{a}{\bf P}^{a}\,

\displaystyle\overline{{\bf Q}}_{i}=\omega(-1)^{i}{\bf Q}^{i}\,

\displaystyle\overline{F}=F\,,

\displaystyle\text{(physical kinematics)},

(110a)

\displaystyle\overline{{\bf P}}_{a}=\frac{F}{\mu}|\epsilon_{ab}|{\bf P}_{b}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{{\bf Q}}_{i}=-\frac{1}{F}|\epsilon_{ij}|{\bf Q}_{j}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{F}=\frac{1}{F}\,,

\displaystyle\text{(mirror kinematics)}.

(110b)

With these reality conditions,
\hat{T}_{a,s}
(
96a
) are real up to the gauge factor
\frac{\bar{U}^{[-2-s]}\bar{U}^{[-2+s]}}{U^{[2+s]}U^{[2-s]}}
, and
\hat{Y}_{a,s}
are real.

Now we comment on concrete examples. The simplest possible solution to a twisted
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
Q-system, and so equivalently to the T-system, is the
character solution
. In this case, all Q-functions are simply proportional to the twist factors,
\mathcal{Q}_{A|I}\propto\left(\frac{\mathtt{x}_{A}}{\mathtt{y}_{I}}\right)^{-\mathtt{i}u}
. Using the unimodularity condition
\mathtt{x}_{1}\mathtt{x}_{2}/\mathtt{y}_{1}\mathtt{y}_{2}=1
, it follows that all T- and Y-functions do not depend on
u
. Furthermore
Gromov:2010vb
,
Kazakov:2015efa

T_{a,s}\propto\Delta\,\chi_{a,s}\,,

(111)

where
\chi_{a,s}(G)
is the character and
\Delta=\det\frac{1}{\mathtt{x}_{a}-\mathtt{y}_{i}}
is the Cauchy double alternant. Also, using additionally
\mathtt{x}_{1}\mathtt{x}_{2}=\mathtt{y}_{1}\mathtt{y}_{2}=1
imposed by
{\bf P}\mu
-equations, we observe that Hodge duality acts trivially on the character solution:
T^{*}_{a,s}=T_{a,s}

26
26

26

If no constraints on
\mathtt{x}_{1},\mathtt{x}_{2},\mathtt{y}_{1},\mathtt{y}_{2}
are imposed, T-functions of the character solution are of the form
T_{a,s}=(\frac{\mathtt{x}_{1}\mathtt{x}_{2}}{\mathtt{y}_{1}\mathtt{y}_{2}})^{-\mathtt{i}u}T^{\text{const}}_{a,s}
with
T^{\text{const}}_{a,s}
being independent of
u
. Hodge duality acts non-trivially by sending
T^{\text{const}}_{a,s}(\mathtt{x}_{a},\mathtt{y}_{i})\rightarrow T^{\text{const}}_{a,s}(\frac{1}{\mathtt{x}_{a}},\frac{1}{\mathtt{y}_{i}})
which corresponds to the map from a covariant to the corresponding contra-variant representation.
. There exists a natural basis for the Q-functions of the character solution:
\mathcal{Q}_{a|\emptyset}=\mathtt{x}_{a}^{-\mathtt{i}u},\,\mathcal{Q}_{\emptyset|i}=\mathtt{y}_{i}^{\mathtt{i}u}
. If
\mathtt{x}_{1},\mathtt{y}_{1}
are real, the basis (
109
) is defined by
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{a|\emptyset}=\mathcal{Q}_{a|\emptyset},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{\emptyset|i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega}}\mathcal{Q}_{\emptyset|i}
. If instead
\mathtt{x}_{1},\mathtt{y}_{1}
are phases,
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{\pm|\emptyset}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathcal{Q}_{1|\emptyset}\pm\mathtt{i}\mathcal{Q}_{2|\emptyset}),\,\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{\emptyset|\pm}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\omega}}(\mathcal{Q}_{\emptyset|1}\pm\mathtt{i}\mathcal{Q}_{\emptyset|2})
gives the basis (
109
).

Analytic properties of T-system (and of some of Q-functions) emerging from TBA were derived in
Cavaglia:2015nta
. Our results perfectly match and further complement those findings thus demonstrating that the monodromy bootstrap works also for derivation of QSC’s describing TBA equations. Comparing the expressions for
Y_{1,1}Y_{2,2}
, equation
(3.1)
in
Cavaglia:2015nta
and (
100
), we see that the relevant source term is
F={F_{\text{exp}}}(-\frac{\mathtt{i}}{T})
for the finite-temperature Hubbard model. To match other expressions in
Cavaglia:2015nta
with ours, we should identify

\displaystyle(-1)^{-\mathtt{i}u}{\bf P}^{\mathtt{H}}_{+}\propto\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1}\,,

\displaystyle(-1)^{\mathtt{i}u}{\bf P}^{\mathtt{H}}_{-}\propto\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{2}\,,

\displaystyle{\bf P}^{\mathtt{V}}_{+}\propto\sqrt{F}\,\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}_{2}\,,

\displaystyle{\bf P}^{\mathtt{V}}_{-}\propto\sqrt{F}\,\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}\vphantom{\hat{\mathbf{q}}}_{1}\,

(112)

as single-valued functions of Zhukovsky variable. The proportionality
\propto
used in this matching is simply proportionality up to a complex number. Clearly
\bar{F}=\frac{1}{F}
and furthermore the Q-functions obey conjugation properties (
109
)
27
27

27

This reality property as stated has only been verified for the ground state, see the resolvent parametrisation in section 4.3 of
Cavaglia:2015nta
, and with purely real twist.
.

T-system is known to emerge not only as a result of TBA but also in algebraic Bethe Ansatz for spin chains. Indeed, T-system exists for any
F
and we already saw that
F=x^{M_{\theta}}
describes the finite-size Hubbard model; an equivalent statement was made in
Cavaglia:2015nta
. The choice
F={F_{\text{ext}}}
also allows building T-systems, now for inhomogeneous Hubbard models discussed in previous sections.

In the spin chain case, T-functions are expected to have interpretation of transfer matrices. For a centrally extended
{\mathsf{su}}(2|2)
spin, eigenvalues for the transfer matrix
T_{1,1}
were proposed in
Beisert:2006qh
and later derived from algebraic Bethe Ansatz in
MartinMello
. Our expressions for
\hat{T}_{1,1}
match these eigenvalues up to redefinitions and an overall gauge prefactor.

The basis which realise (
109
) is in general not appropriate for studying spin chains, the reason being that there exist a distinguished basis with
{\bf P}_{a}
having different asymptotic. To match this basis we should pick
\vec{n}\cdot\vec{\sigma}=\sigma^{1}
. This gives
\overline{\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{a}}=(-1)^{a}\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{a}
and
\overline{\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{i}}=(-1)^{i}\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{i}\frac{1}{FU^{[2]}\bar{U}^{[-2]}}
. The extra factors in the conjugation property of
\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{i}
suggest redefining
\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{i}
. Indeed, for an
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
spin chain this is exactly what we did in (
81
), this definition leads to purely imaginary/real
\mathbf{q}_{i}(x)
. Notice that to have a sensible definition of reality we need to restrict to all twists being phases and pick
y^{\pm}_{k}
appropriately so that
\bar{F}=\frac{1}{F}
.
\hat{T}_{a,s}
, including
\hat{T}_{1,1}
, are not real, this is the typical case for
e.g.
rational spin chains if the symmetry is larger than
\mathsf{SU}(2)
.

7
Asymptotic Q-systems for the massive sector of AdS
3
/CFT
2
integrability

The relevant to us study of integrability in AdS
3
/CFT
2
correspondence was initiated in
Babichenko:2009dk
where a set of Bethe equations was proposed. An important feature in AdS
3
/CFT
2
is that the isometry group of space-time splits as a product and one obtains two coupled sets of Bethe equations. Using the S-matrices found in
SMatricesAdS3
it was realised in AdS
{}_{3}\times
S
{}^{3}\times
S
{}^{3}\times
S
1

Borsato:2012ss
that the relative grading between the two sets of Bethe equations should be different. This analysis was then extended
Borsato:2013qpa
to AdS
{}_{3}\times
S
{}^{3}\times
T
4
where the following set of Bethe equations were found

\displaystyle 1=\prod^{M_{2}}_{j=1}\frac{y_{i,k}-x^{-}_{j}}{y_{i,k}-x^{+}_{j}}\prod^{M_{\bar{2}}}_{j=1}\frac{1-\frac{1}{y_{i,k}\overline{x}^{+}_{j}}}{1-\frac{1}{y_{i,k}\overline{x}^{-}_{j}}}\,,

(113a)

\displaystyle 1=\prod^{M_{\bar{2}}}_{j=1}\frac{y_{\bar{i},k}-\overline{x}^{+}_{j}}{y_{\bar{i},k}-\overline{x}^{-}_{j}}\prod^{M_{2}}_{j=1}\frac{1-\frac{1}{y_{\bar{i},k}x^{-}_{j}}}{1-\frac{1}{y_{\bar{i},k}x^{+}_{j}}}\,,

(113b)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{x^{+}_{k}}{x^{-}_{k}}\right)^{L}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}^{M_{2}}\frac{u_{k}-u_{j}+i}{u_{k}-u_{j}-i}\left(\sigma^{\bullet\bullet}(x_{k},x_{j})\right)^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{M_{\bar{2}}}\frac{1-\frac{1}{x^{+}_{k}\overline{x}^{+}_{j}}}{1-\frac{1}{x^{-}_{k}\overline{x}^{-}_{j}}}\frac{1-\frac{1}{x^{+}_{k}\overline{x}^{-}_{j}}}{1-\frac{1}{x^{-}_{k}\overline{x}^{+}_{j}}}\left(\hat{\sigma}^{\bullet\bullet}(x_{k},\overline{x}_{j})\right)^{2}\times

(113c)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\prod_{j=1}^{M_{1}}\frac{x^{-}_{k}-y_{1,j}}{x^{+}_{k}-y_{1,j}}\prod_{j=1}^{M_{3}}\frac{x^{-}_{k}-y_{3,j}}{x^{+}_{k}-y_{3,j}}\prod_{j=1}^{M_{\bar{1}}}\frac{1-\frac{1}{x^{-}_{k}y_{\bar{1},j}}}{1-\frac{1}{x^{+}_{k}y_{\bar{1},j}}}\prod_{j=1}^{M_{\bar{3}}}\frac{1-\frac{1}{x^{-}_{k}y_{\bar{3},j}}}{1-\frac{1}{x^{+}_{k}y_{\bar{3},j}}}\,,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\overline{x}^{+}_{k}}{\overline{x}^{-}_{k}}\right)^{L}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
