j\neq k\end{subarray}}^{M_{\bar{2}}}\frac{\overline{x}^{-}_{k}-\overline{x}^{+}_{j}}{\overline{x}^{+}_{k}-\overline{x}^{-}_{j}}\frac{1-\frac{1}{\overline{x}^{+}_{k}\overline{x}^{-}_{j}}}{1-\frac{1}{\overline{x}^{-}_{k}\overline{x}^{+}_{j}}}\left(\sigma^{\bullet\bullet}(\overline{x}_{k},\overline{x}_{j})\right)^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{M_{2}}\frac{1-\frac{1}{\overline{x}^{-}_{k}x^{-}_{j}}}{1-\frac{1}{\overline{x}^{-}_{k}x^{+}_{j}}}\frac{1-\frac{1}{\overline{x}^{+}_{k}x^{-}_{j}}}{1-\frac{1}{\overline{x}^{+}_{k}x^{+}_{j}}}\left(\hat{\sigma}^{\bullet\bullet}(\overline{x}_{k},x_{j})\right)^{2}\times

(113d)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\prod_{j=1}^{M_{\bar{1}}}\frac{\overline{x}^{+}_{k}-y_{\bar{1},j}}{\overline{x}^{-}_{k}-y_{\bar{1},j}}\prod_{j=1}^{M_{\bar{3}}}\frac{\overline{x}^{+}_{k}-y_{\bar{3},j}}{\overline{x}^{-}_{k}-y_{\bar{3},j}}\prod_{j=1}^{M_{1}}\frac{1-\frac{1}{\overline{x}^{+}_{k}y_{1,j}}}{1-\frac{1}{\overline{x}^{-}_{k}y_{1,j}}}\prod_{j=1}^{M_{3}}\frac{1-\frac{1}{\overline{x}^{+}_{k}y_{3,j}}}{1-\frac{1}{\overline{x}^{+}_{k}y_{3,j}}}\,.

Here
x_{j}
and
\overline{x}_{j}
are distinct momentum-carrying roots;
y_{i,k},y_{\bar{i},k},i=1,3
are auxiliary roots; and
\sigma^{\bullet\bullet}
and
\hat{\sigma}^{\bullet\bullet}
are dressing phases. These phases are constrained by crossing equations considered and solved in
Borsato:2013hoa
.

An important ingredient in AdS
3
/CFT
2
correspondence are massless modes, there also exist a full set of Bethe equations incorporating them
Borsato:2016xns
.

One should expect that Bethe equations can be obtained from QQ-relations and that there thus should exist a Q-system of type
\mathsf{PSU}(1,1|2)^{2}
to reflect the symmetry of the full theory. In the language of Q-systems the
\mathsf{P}
of
\mathsf{PSU}
is the zero central charge condition and the specified non-compact real form means that the monodromy properties of fermionic Q-functions,
\mathcal{Q}_{\emptyset|i}
, should be compatible with non-integer asymptotics.

Bethe equations describe the spectrum only asymptotically and hence the Q-system that reproduces them should do the same, we shall refer to it as the asymptotic Q-system. Given the success in AdS
5
/CFT
4

Gromov:2014caa
and AdS
4
/CFT
3

Bombardelli:2017vhk
integrability, one could expect that the asymptotic Q-system might be improved to become a quantum spectral curve describing the spectrum of the theory at finite value of the coupling constant and charges.
In Section
5
, we suggested two non-trivial
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)\times\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
quantum spectral curves which would be natural candidates for this role. Because we are interested only in a system with zero central charge, the two quantum spectral curves cannot be distinguished, see Section
4.1
. We therefore have the unique QSC coming from the monodromy bootstrap. It is the natural candidate for exact QSC of AdS
3
/CFT
2
integrability.

AdS
3
/CFT
2
QSC Conjecture:
The QSC of AdS
3

\times
S
3

\times
T
4
is given by model C in the special case of
F=\bar{F}=\det\mu_{{\dot{a}}a}=\det\bar{\mu}_{a{\dot{a}}}=\det\omega^{i\dot{i}}=\det\bar{\omega}^{\dot{i}i}=1
,
{{\rm p}_{\omega}}={{\rm p}_{\mu}}={{\rm p}_{\bar{\omega}}}={{\rm p}_{\bar{\mu}}}=0
and a power-like asymptotic behaviour of Q-functions at infinity.

In component form,
{\bf P}\mu
-system (
68
) is

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\mu}}\vphantom{\bar{\mu}}_{a{\dot{a}}}-\bar{\mu}_{a{\dot{a}}}=\frac{1}{r}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathbf{P}}\vphantom{\mathbf{P}}_{a}\bar{\mathbf{P}}_{{\dot{a}}}-\frac{1}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}}\mathbf{P}_{a}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\mathbf{P}}}\vphantom{\bar{\mathbf{P}}}_{{\dot{a}}}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{{\dot{a}}a}-\mu_{{\dot{a}}a}=\frac{1}{\bar{r}}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\mathbf{P}}}\vphantom{\bar{\mathbf{P}}}_{{\dot{a}}}\mathbf{P}_{a}-\frac{1}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{r}}\vphantom{\bar{r}}}\bar{\mathbf{P}}_{{\dot{a}}}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathbf{P}}\vphantom{\mathbf{P}}_{a}\,,

(114a)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}=r\bar{\mu}_{a{\dot{a}}}\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{{\dot{a}}}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{a}=-\frac{1}{r}\bar{\mathbf{P}}_{{\dot{a}}}\bar{\mu}^{{\dot{a}}a}\,,

(114b)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\mathbf{P}}}\vphantom{\bar{\mathbf{P}}}_{{\dot{a}}}=\bar{r}\mu_{{\dot{a}}a}{\bf P}^{a}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\mathbf{P}}}\vphantom{\bar{\mathbf{P}}}^{{\dot{a}}}=-\frac{1}{\bar{r}}{\bf P}_{a}\mu^{a{\dot{a}}}\,.

(114c)

{\bf Q}\omega
-system reads

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\omega}\vphantom{\omega}^{i\dot{j}}-\omega^{i\dot{j}}=\frac{1}{r}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf Q}}\vphantom{{\bf Q}}^{i}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}^{\dot{j}}-\frac{1}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}}{\bf Q}^{i}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\mathbf{Q}}}\vphantom{\bar{\mathbf{Q}}}^{\dot{j}}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\omega}}\vphantom{\bar{\omega}}^{\dot{i}j}-\bar{\omega}^{\dot{i}j}=\frac{1}{\bar{r}}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\mathbf{Q}}}\vphantom{\bar{\mathbf{Q}}}^{\dot{i}}\mathbf{Q}^{j}-\frac{1}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{r}}\vphantom{\bar{r}}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}^{\dot{i}}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathbf{Q}}\vphantom{\mathbf{Q}}^{j}\,,

(115a)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf Q}}\vphantom{{\bf Q}}_{i}=-\frac{1}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{r}}\vphantom{\bar{r}}}\bar{\omega}_{i\dot{j}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}^{\dot{j}}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf Q}}\vphantom{{\bf Q}}^{i}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{r}}\vphantom{\bar{r}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\dot{j}}\bar{\omega}^{\dot{j}i}\,,

(115b)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\mathbf{Q}}}\vphantom{\bar{\mathbf{Q}}}_{\dot{i}}=-\frac{1}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}}\omega_{\dot{i}j}{\bf Q}^{j}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\mathbf{Q}}}\vphantom{\bar{\mathbf{Q}}}^{\dot{i}}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{r}\vphantom{r}{\bf Q}_{j}\omega^{j\dot{i}}\,.

(115c)

For systems of
\mathsf{PSU}
-type,
r
and
\bar{r}
can be eliminated using a gauge transformation. Indeed, from
\mathbf{P}_{a}\mathbf{P}^{a}=0
it follows that
\mathbf{P}_{a}=\alpha\epsilon_{ab}\mathbf{P}^{b}
. We can compute then

r\bar{\mu}_{a{\dot{a}}}\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{{\dot{a}}}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\alpha}\vphantom{\alpha}\epsilon_{ab}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}^{b}=-\frac{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\alpha}\vphantom{\alpha}}{r}\epsilon_{ab}\bar{\mathbf{P}}_{{\dot{a}}}\bar{\mu}^{{\dot{a}}b}=-\frac{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\alpha}\vphantom{\alpha}}{r}\epsilon_{ab}\epsilon^{{\dot{a}}\dot{b}}\epsilon^{bc}\bar{\mu}_{c\dot{b}}\bar{\mathbf{P}}_{{\dot{a}}}=\frac{\bar{\alpha}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\alpha}\vphantom{\alpha}}{r}\bar{\mu}_{a{\dot{a}}}\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{{\dot{a}}}\,,

(116)

and so

\displaystyle r^{2}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\alpha}\vphantom{\alpha}\bar{\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{r}^{2}=\alpha\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\alpha}}\vphantom{\bar{\alpha}}\,.

(117)

Analyticity of
\alpha
was studied in Section
4.1
. It is possible to pick a gauge such that
r=\bar{r}=\pm 1
, and we can enforce the plus sign for
r,\bar{r}
by changing the sign of
\mu,\bar{\mu}
and
\omega,\bar{\omega}
.

Setting
r=1
is basically the same as enforcing re-definitions like
{\bf P}_{1}\to\sqrt{{\bf P}_{1}{\bf P}^{2}}
. This may introduce extra square root branch points (that are typically not correlated to Zhukovsky ones). To avoid this potentially unwanted drawback in explicit computations, we shall keep
r
unfixed.

In this section we shall demonstrate that the proposed exact system is compatible, in the asymptotic limit, with the above Bethe equations and furthermore we will attempt to gain better understanding of how the square root property may fail. Thus we will assume the existence of two exact Q-systems,
\mathcal{Q},\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
with a well-defined asymptotic limit reproducing (
113
). We will use the font
\mathtt{Q}
to distinguish asymptotic Q-functions from Q-functions of a full exact theory. In terms of
\mathtt{Q}
, (
113
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{-}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}}}{\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{+}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}}}\bigg\rvert_{\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}|\emptyset}=0}=\frac{\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{1|1}}{\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{1|1}}\bigg\rvert_{\mathtt{Q}_{\emptyset|1}=0}=1\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{-}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}}}{\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{+}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}}}\bigg\rvert_{\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}\dot{2}}=0}=\frac{\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{1|1}}{\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{1|1}}\bigg\rvert_{\mathtt{Q}_{12|1}=0}=1\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathtt{Q}^{[2]}_{1|1}}{\mathtt{Q}^{[-2]}_{1|1}}\frac{\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{\emptyset|1}\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{12|1}}{\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{\emptyset|1}\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{12|1}}\bigg\rvert_{\mathtt{Q}_{1|1}=0}=-1\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{[-2]}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}}}{\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{[2]}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}}}\frac{\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{+}_{\dot{1}|\emptyset}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{+}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}\dot{2}}}{\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{-}_{\dot{1}|\emptyset}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{-}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}\dot{2}}}\bigg\rvert_{\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}}=0}=-1\,.

There also exist different versions of Bethe equations corresponding to other gradings of
\mathsf{PSU}(1,1|2)
Kac-Dynkin diagram, see
DifferentGradingPSU
. Using also these additional equations, or equivalently fermionic QQ-relations, we find the asymptotic Q-functions

\displaystyle\mathtt{Q}_{1|1}\propto\mathbb{Q}\,\mathtt{f}^{+}\bar{\mathtt{f}}^{+}\,,

\displaystyle\mathtt{Q}_{12|12}=1\,,

(118)

\displaystyle\mathtt{Q}_{1|\emptyset}\propto x^{-L/2}B_{-}R_{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{y}\vphantom{y}_{1}}\sigma\hat{\bar{\sigma}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathtt{Q}_{1|12}\propto x^{-L/2}B_{+}R_{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{y}\vphantom{y}_{3}}\sigma\hat{\bar{\sigma}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathtt{Q}_{\emptyset|1}\propto x^{L/2}\frac{\bar{\mathtt{f}}}{\bar{B}_{+}}\mathtt{f}R_{y_{1}}\frac{1}{\sigma\hat{\bar{\sigma}}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathtt{Q}_{12|1}\propto x^{L/2}\frac{\bar{\mathtt{f}}}{\bar{B}_{+}}\mathtt{f}^{[2]}R_{y_{3}}\frac{1}{\sigma\hat{\bar{\sigma}}}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}}\propto\bar{\mathbb{Q}}\,\bar{\mathtt{f}}^{+}\mathtt{f}^{+}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}\dot{2}|\dot{1}\dot{2}}=1\,,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}|\emptyset}\propto x^{-L/2}\bar{B}_{-}B_{y_{1}}\hat{\sigma}\bar{\sigma}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}\dot{2}}\propto x^{-L/2}\bar{B}_{+}B_{y_{3}}\hat{\sigma}\bar{\sigma}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\emptyset|\dot{1}}\propto x^{L/2}\frac{\mathtt{f}}{B_{+}}\bar{\mathtt{f}}B_{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{y}\vphantom{y}_{1}}\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}\bar{\sigma}}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}\dot{2}|\dot{1}}\propto x^{L/2}\frac{\mathtt{f}}{B_{+}}\bar{\mathtt{f}}^{[2]}B_{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{y}\vphantom{y}_{3}}\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}\bar{\sigma}}\,.

The notation, mirroring the standard notation from AdS
5
/CFT
4
, is as follows for objects related to momentum-carrying nodes

\displaystyle B_{(\pm)}=\prod_{j=1}^{M_{2}}\sqrt{\frac{{\mbox{h}}}{2x^{\mp}_{j}}}(\frac{1}{x}-x^{\mp}_{j})\,,

\displaystyle\bar{B}_{(\pm)}=\prod_{j=1}^{M_{\bar{2}}}\sqrt{\frac{{\mbox{h}}}{2\overline{x}^{\mp}_{j}}}(\frac{1}{x}-\overline{x}^{\mp}_{j})\,,

(119a)

\displaystyle R_{(\pm)}=\prod_{j=1}^{M_{2}}\sqrt{\frac{{\mbox{h}}}{2x^{\mp}_{j}}}(x-x^{\mp}_{j})\,,

\displaystyle\bar{R}_{\pm}=\prod^{M_{\bar{2}}}_{j=1}\sqrt{\frac{{\mbox{h}}}{2\overline{x}^{\mp}_{j}}}(x-\overline{x}^{\mp}_{j})\,,

(119b)

\displaystyle\mathtt{f}=\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{B^{[2n]}_{(+)}}{B^{[2n]}_{(-)}}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathtt{f}}=\prod^{\infty}_{n=0}\frac{\bar{B}^{[2n]}_{(+)}}{\bar{B}^{[2n]}_{(-)}}\,.

(119c)

For the auxiliary roots
y_{1,k},y_{3,k},y_{\bar{1},k},y_{\bar{3},k}
, we have used

\displaystyle R_{y_{i}}=\prod_{k=1}^{M_{i}}(x-y_{i,k})\prod_{k=1}^{M_{\bar{i}}}(\frac{1}{x}-y_{\bar{i},k})\,,

\displaystyle B_{y_{i}}=\prod_{k=1}^{M_{\bar{i}}}(x-y_{\bar{i},k})\prod_{k=1}^{M_{i}}(\frac{1}{x}-y_{i,k})\,,

(120)

so that in particular
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{R}\vphantom{R}_{y_{i}}=B_{y_{i}}
. The auxiliary roots are not independent, for example QQ-relation
\mathtt{Q}_{1|\emptyset}\mathtt{Q}_{\emptyset|1}=\mathtt{Q}_{1|1}^{+}-\mathtt{Q}_{1|1}^{-}=-\mathtt{Q}_{1|12}\mathtt{Q}_{12|1}
enforces the relation
R_{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{y}\vphantom{y}_{1}}R_{y_{1}}\propto R_{y_{3}}R_{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{y}\vphantom{y}_{3}}
. Also note that we have assumed the zero-momentum condition

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{M_{2}}\frac{x^{+}_{k}}{x^{-}_{k}}\prod_{k=1}^{M_{\bar{2}}}\frac{\overline{x}^{+}_{k}}{\overline{x}^{-}_{k}}=1\,

(121)

in order to fully reproduce (
113
).

For convenience we will also use
\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}\equiv\mathtt{f}\bar{\mathtt{f}}
. Finally we note that the dressing phases can be computed as

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{+}(x)}{\sigma^{-}(x)}\equiv\prod_{j=1}^{M_{2}}\sigma^{\bullet\bullet}(x,x_{k})\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\sigma}^{+}(x)}{\hat{\sigma}^{-}(x)}\equiv\prod_{j=1}^{M_{2}}\hat{\sigma}^{\bullet\bullet}(x,x_{k})\,,

(122)

with the natural generalisation for the right-moving quantities. We did not impose any constraints on
\sigma
and
\hat{\sigma}
so far, deriving crossing equations for them from comparison with the exact QSC will be an important check.

We now turn to the proposed exact quantum spectral curve. From symmetry principles
Gromov:2014caa
, Q-functions in the large-
u
limit should scale as

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{a}\sim u^{-\hat{\lambda}_{a}^{L}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}^{a}\sim u^{\hat{\lambda}^{L}_{a}-1}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{i}\sim u^{-\hat{\nu}_{i}^{L}-1}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}^{i}\sim u^{\hat{\nu}^{L}_{i}}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{P}}_{{\dot{a}}}\sim u^{-\hat{\lambda}^{R}_{a}}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\dot{a}}\sim u^{\hat{\lambda}^{R}_{a}-1}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\dot{i}}}\sim u^{-\hat{\nu}^{R}_{i}-1}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{Q}}^{{\dot{i}}}\sim u^{\hat{\nu}^{R}_{i}}\,,

(123)

where
\hat{\lambda},\hat{\nu}
are shifted weights of the left and the right symmetry algebra. In the current
\mathsf{SU}(2)\times\mathsf{SL}(2)
grading, we find
\hat{\lambda}^{a}_{1}=\lambda^{a}_{1}+\delta_{a,L},\hat{\lambda}^{a}_{2}=\lambda^{a}_{2}+\delta_{a,R},\hat{\nu}^{a}_{1}=\nu^{a}_{1}-\delta_{a,L},\hat{\nu}^{a}_{2}=\nu^{2}_{a}-\delta_{a,R}
, see Appendix C in
Gromov:2014caa
for more details. These expressions also follow from quasi-momentum that defines the classical spectral curve of AdS
3
/CFT
2

Babichenko:2014yaa
. We can express the weights in terms of the energy and the spin,
\Delta,S
of AdS
3
as well as the two angular momenta of S
3
,
J,K
:

\displaystyle\lambda^{L}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(J+K)+\Lambda^{L}\,,

\displaystyle\lambda^{L}_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}(J+K)+\Lambda^{L}\,,

\displaystyle\nu_{1}^{L}=-\frac{1}{2}(\Delta+S)-\Lambda^{L}\,,

\displaystyle\nu^{L}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta+S)-\Lambda^{L}\,,

\displaystyle\lambda^{R}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(J-K)+\Lambda^{R}\,,

\displaystyle\lambda^{R}_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}(J-K)+\Lambda^{R}\,,

\displaystyle\nu^{R}_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}(\Delta-S)-\Lambda^{R}\,,

\displaystyle\nu^{R}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta-S)-\Lambda^{R}\,.

Explicit expressions for charges in terms of Bethe roots
Borsato:2013qpa
set
\Lambda^{L}=\frac{1}{2}(M_{1}-M_{3}),\,\Lambda^{R}=-\frac{1}{2}(M_{\bar{1}}-M_{\bar{3}})
.

We assume as in
Gromov:2014caa
that the large-
u
scaling dictates, at least on the defining sheet, the large volume scaling. We define the large volume regime as the limit
\Delta\simeq J\rightarrow\infty
. To keep track of the scaling, we introduce the parameter
\epsilon=u^{-\frac{\Delta}{2}}
. Using the explicit scalings given in (
7
), we find

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{1}\sim\mathbf{P}^{2}\sim\epsilon\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{2}\sim\mathbf{P}^{1}\sim\epsilon^{-1}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{1}\sim\mathbf{Q}^{2}\sim\epsilon^{-1}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{2}\sim\mathbf{Q}^{1}\sim\epsilon\,,

(124a)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{P}}_{\dot{1}}\sim\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\dot{2}}\sim\epsilon\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{P}}_{\dot{2}}\sim\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{\dot{1}}\sim\epsilon^{-1}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\dot{1}}\sim\bar{\mathbf{Q}}^{\dot{2}}\sim\epsilon^{-1}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\dot{2}}\sim\bar{\mathbf{Q}}^{\dot{1}}\sim\epsilon\,.

(124b)

Use the relations

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}_{a{\dot{a}}}=-\omega^{i{\dot{i}}}\,\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{a|i}\,\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{-}_{{\dot{a}}|{\dot{i}}}\,,

\displaystyle\mu_{{\dot{a}}a}=-\bar{\omega}^{{\dot{i}}i}\,\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{-}_{{\dot{a}}|{\dot{i}}}\,\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{a|i}\,.

(125)

Now, since
\omega^{i{\dot{i}}},\bar{\omega}^{{\dot{i}}i}
are periodic, they should scale as constants at large
u
or decrease exponentially. At the same time, from the scaling of Q-functions and the QQ-relation
\mathbf{P}_{a}\mathbf{Q}_{i}=\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}^{+}-\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{a|i}
we find that
\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}\sim u^{-\hat{\lambda}^{L}_{a}-\hat{\nu}^{L}_{i}}
. In particular

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{1|1}\sim 1\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{1|2}\sim\epsilon^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{2|1}\sim\epsilon^{-2}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{2|2}\sim 1\,,

(126)

with the same scaling for
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\dot{a}}|{\dot{i}}}
. Plugging this into (
125
), we deduce

\displaystyle(\bar{\mu}_{\text{as}})_{a\dot{a}}=-(\omega_{\text{as}})^{1\dot{1}}\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{a|1}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{-}_{\dot{a}|\dot{1}}\,,

\displaystyle{(\mu_{\text{as}})}_{{\dot{a}}a}=-(\bar{\omega}_{\text{as}})^{\dot{1}1}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{-}_{{\dot{a}}|\dot{1}}\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{a|1}\,,

(127)

where we write
(\bar{\mu}_{\text{as}})_{a\dot{a}}
for the asymptotic versions of
\bar{\mu}_{a\dot{a}}
and so on. It is reassuring to see that from the scaling of
\mathcal{Q}_{a|i}
it follows that

(\bar{\mu}_{\text{as}})_{1\dot{1}}\sim u^{-\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{L}-\hat{\nu}_{1}^{L}-\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{R}-\hat{\nu}^{R}_{1}}\sim u^{\Delta-J}\,,

(128)

and, using the definition of
\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}
, one reproduces the standard expression for the energy in terms of Bethe roots
Gromov:2011cx
. We focus now on
{(\mu_{\text{as}})}_{\dot{1}1},(\bar{\mu}_{\text{as}})_{1\dot{1}}
as they depend on
\mathtt{Q}
-functions that appear directly in asymptotic Bethe equations. From periodicity of
\omega
we find

\frac{{(\bar{\mu}_{\text{as}})}^{[2]}_{1\dot{1}}}{(\bar{\mu}_{\text{as}})_{1\dot{1}}}=\frac{\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{1|1}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{+}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}}}{\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{1|1}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}^{-}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}}}=\frac{\mathbb{Q}^{+}\bar{\mathbb{Q}}^{+}}{\mathbb{Q}^{-}\bar{\mathbb{Q}}^{-}}\left(\frac{\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{[2]}}{\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}}\right)^{2}\,,

(129)

where we have used the explicit expressions for the asymptotic Q-functions in the second expression. It follows that
(\bar{\mu}_{\text{as}})_{1\dot{1}}
and
{(\mu_{\text{as}})}_{\dot{1}1}
must have square-root branch cuts to match the proposed asymptotic Q-system.

We also have

\displaystyle(\bar{\mu}_{\text{as}})_{1\dot{1}}\propto{(\mu_{\text{as}})}_{\dot{1}1}\propto\mathbb{Q}^{-}\bar{\mathbb{Q}}^{-}(\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}})(\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{*})^{[-2]}\,,

\displaystyle(\bar{\omega}_{\text{as}})^{\dot{1}1}\propto(\omega_{\text{as}})^{1\dot{1}}\propto\frac{\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{*[-2]}}{\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}}\,,

(130)

where
\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{*}=\prod\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{B^{[-2n]}_{(-)}}{B^{[-2n]}_{(+)}}\frac{\bar{B}^{[-2n]}_{(-)}}{\bar{B}^{[-2n]}_{(+)}}
.
These expressions are clearly similar to those in (
36
) but
\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}
is not related to any gauge transformation.

We next compute the asymptotic approximation of exact
{\bf P}
-functions on their next-to-the defining Riemann sheets (in the physical kinematics). It will be instructive to calculate both clock-wise and counter-clock-wise:

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}=r\bar{\mu}_{a{\dot{a}}}\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{{\dot{a}}}=-r\omega^{i{\dot{i}}}\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{a|i}\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{-}_{{\dot{a}}|{\dot{i}}}\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{{\dot{a}}}=-r\omega^{i{\dot{i}}}\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{a|i}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\dot{i}}}\sim r(\omega_{\text{as}})^{1\dot{1}}\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{a|1}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\emptyset|\dot{1}}\,,

(131a)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}_{a}=-\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{r}}\vphantom{\bar{r}}\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{{\dot{a}}}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mu}\vphantom{\mu}_{{\dot{a}}a}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{r}}\vphantom{\bar{r}}\bar{\omega}^{{\dot{i}}i}\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}_{{\dot{a}}|{\dot{i}}}\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{a|i}\bar{\mathbf{P}}^{{\dot{a}}}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{r}}\vphantom{\bar{r}}\bar{\omega}^{{\dot{i}}i}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{{\dot{i}}}\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{a|i}\sim\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{r}}\vphantom{\bar{r}}(\bar{\omega}_{\text{as}})^{\dot{1}1}\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{a|1}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\emptyset|\dot{1}}\,.

(131b)

Can we assume
\big(\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{{\bf P}}\vphantom{{\bf P}}\big)_{\rm as}=\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{({\bf P}_{\rm as})}\vphantom{({\bf P}_{\rm as})}
? In general, taking the asymptotic limit does not commute with the analytic continuation across the cut. However we expect this to be the case for
{\bf P}_{1}=\mathcal{Q}_{1|\emptyset}
and
{\bf P}^{2}\propto\mathcal{Q}_{1|12}
because these Q-functions are small in the asymptotic limit. In Stokes-type phenomena (and crossing the branch have similar features), analytic continuation of the small solution across the Stokes line can be trusted, while analytic continuation of a large solution cannot be.

We summarise

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathtt{Q}}\vphantom{\mathtt{Q}}_{1|\emptyset}\propto r(\omega_{\text{as}})^{1\dot{1}}\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{1|1}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\emptyset|\dot{1}}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathtt{Q}}\vphantom{\mathtt{Q}}_{1|12}\propto\frac{1}{r}(\omega_{\text{as}})^{1\dot{1}}\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{1|1}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}\dot{2}|\dot{1}}\,,

(132a)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathtt{Q}}\vphantom{\mathtt{Q}}_{1|\emptyset}\propto\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{r}}\vphantom{\bar{r}}(\bar{\omega}_{\text{as}})^{\dot{1}1}\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{1|1}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\emptyset|1}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathtt{Q}}\vphantom{\mathtt{Q}}_{1|12}\propto\frac{1}{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{r}}\vphantom{\bar{r}}}(\bar{\omega}_{\text{as}})^{\dot{1}1}\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{1|1}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}\dot{2}|\dot{1}}\,,

(132b)

and with the clear generalisation for
\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}|\emptyset},\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}|\dot{1}\dot{2}}
.

We use these expressions to compute the analytic continuation of
\sigma\hat{\bar{\sigma}}
. It is useful to combine
\mathtt{Q}_{1|0}\mathtt{Q}_{1|12}
and
\mathtt{Q}_{1|\emptyset}\mathtt{Q}_{1|12}
because factors of
r,\bar{r}
will cancel then. This gives

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathtt{Q}}\vphantom{\mathtt{Q}}_{1|\emptyset}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathtt{Q}}\vphantom{\mathtt{Q}}_{1|12}\propto\left((\omega_{\text{as}})^{1\dot{1}}\mathtt{Q}_{1|1}^{-}\right)^{2}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\emptyset|\dot{1}}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}\dot{2}|\dot{1}}=\left((\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{*})^{[-2]}\right)^{2}(\mathbb{Q}^{-})^{2}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\emptyset|\dot{1}}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}\dot{2}|\dot{1}}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathtt{Q}}\vphantom{\mathtt{Q}}_{1|\emptyset}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathtt{Q}}\vphantom{\mathtt{Q}}_{1|12}\propto\left((\omega_{\text{as}})^{1\dot{1}}\mathtt{Q}_{1|1}^{+}\right)^{2}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\emptyset|\dot{1}}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}\dot{2}|\dot{1}}=\left((\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{*})^{[-2]}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{R_{(+)}}{R_{(-)}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\bar{B}_{(-)}}{\bar{B}_{(+)}}\right)^{2}(\mathbb{Q}^{-})^{2}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\emptyset|\dot{1}}\overline{\mathtt{Q}}_{\dot{1}\dot{2}|\dot{1}}\,.

We see that these equations are compatible with the relation
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{R_{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{y}\vphantom{y}_{1}}R_{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{y}\vphantom{y}_{3}}}\vphantom{R_{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{y}\vphantom{y}_{1}}R_{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{y}\vphantom{y}_{3}}}=B_{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{y}\vphantom{y}_{1}}B_{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.7014pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{y}\vphantom{y}_{3}}
.
We can then plug in our asymptotic Q-functions to find the following crossing equations for the dressing phases

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{(\sigma\hat{\bar{\sigma}})}\vphantom{(\sigma\hat{\bar{\sigma}})}^{2}(\bar{\sigma}\hat{\sigma})^{2}=(\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{[2]}\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{*[-2]})^{2}\frac{R_{(-)}}{R_{(+)}}\frac{\bar{B}_{(+)}}{\bar{B}_{(-)}}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{(\hat{\sigma}\bar{\sigma})}\vphantom{(\hat{\sigma}\bar{\sigma})}^{2}(\sigma\hat{\bar{\sigma}})^{2}=(\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{[2]}\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{*[-2]})^{2}\frac{\bar{R}_{(-)}}{\bar{R}_{(+)}}\frac{B_{(+)}}{B_{(-)}}\,,

(133)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{(\sigma\hat{\bar{\sigma}})}\vphantom{(\sigma\hat{\bar{\sigma}})}^{2}(\bar{\sigma}\hat{\sigma})^{2}=(\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{[2]}\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{*[-2]})^{2}\frac{R_{(+)}}{R_{(-)}}\frac{\bar{B}_{(-)}}{\bar{B}_{(+)}}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle*$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{(\hat{\sigma}\bar{\sigma})}\vphantom{(\hat{\sigma}\bar{\sigma})}^{2}(\sigma\hat{\bar{\sigma}})^{2}=(\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{[2]}\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{*[-2]})^{2}\frac{\bar{R}_{(+)}}{\bar{R}_{(-)}}\frac{B_{(-)}}{B_{(+)}}\,.

(134)

We notice that the factor of
\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{[2]}\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}^{*[-2]}
is a familiar BES-type of a phase. Absorb this factor by a redefinition:

\displaystyle(\sigma\hat{\bar{\sigma}})^{2}=({\sigma_{\text{BES}}})^{2}\sigma^{(+)}\,,

\displaystyle(\bar{\sigma}\hat{\sigma})^{2}=({\sigma_{\text{BES}}})^{2}\bar{\sigma}^{(+)}

(135)

so that we can summarise the remaining equations as

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\sigma^{(+)}}\vphantom{\sigma^{(+)}}\bar{\sigma}^{(+)}=\left(\frac{R_{(-)}}{R_{(+)}}\frac{\bar{B}_{(+)}}{\bar{B}_{(-)}}\right)^{\eta}\,,

\displaystyle\sigma^{(+)}\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\bar{\sigma}^{(+)}}\vphantom{\bar{\sigma}^{(+)}}=\left(\frac{\bar{R}_{(-)}}{\bar{R}_{(+)}}\frac{B_{(+)}}{B_{(-)}}\right)^{\eta}\,,

(136)

where
\eta=\pm 1
for clock-wise/counter-clock-wise crossing. Clearly we have two different results and we conclude that with our current assumptions the branch-cut cannot be of square root type. Instead if we compute what happens when we cross the cut twice

{\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\approx$}}}}{\sigma^{(+)}}\vphantom{\sigma^{(+)}}}=\left(\frac{B_{(-)}}{B_{(+)}}\frac{\bar{R}_{(+)}}{\bar{R}_{(-)}}\right)^{2\eta}\sigma^{(+)}

(137)

we see that the cut is of a logarithmic type. The crossing equations found here match exactly those of
Borsato:2013hoa
, see also
sax2019singularities
. This is a non-trivial result that supports our conjecture.

Let us now turn to the question of cut structure for
\mu_{{\dot{a}}a}
and
\bar{\mu}_{a{\dot{a}}}
in the asymptotic limit. We already know that
(\bar{\mu}_{\text{as}})_{1\dot{1}},{(\mu_{\text{as}})}_{\dot{1}1}
have square root cuts. However, consider now
(\bar{\mu}_{\text{as}})_{2\dot{1}}
. To compute this object, we need
\mathtt{Q}_{2|1}
which can be obtained from the QQ-relation
\mathtt{Q}_{2|\emptyset}\mathtt{Q}_{\emptyset|1}=\mathtt{Q}_{2|1}^{+}-\mathtt{Q}_{2|1}^{-}
. We find
\mathtt{Q}_{2|\emptyset}
from

\displaystyle\mathtt{Q}_{1|\emptyset}^{+}\,\mathtt{Q}_{2|\emptyset}^{-}-\mathtt{Q}_{1|\emptyset}^{-}\,\mathtt{Q}_{2|\emptyset}^{+}=\mathtt{Q}_{12|\emptyset}\,,

\displaystyle\mathtt{Q}_{12|1}^{+}\,\mathtt{Q}_{1|\emptyset}^{-}-\mathtt{Q}_{12|1}^{-}\,\mathtt{Q}_{1|\emptyset}^{+}=\mathtt{Q}_{1|1}\mathtt{Q}_{12|\emptyset}\,,

(138)

Combining these equations and using
f^{+}\,g^{-}-f^{-}\,g^{+}=h\implies f=-g\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{h}{g^{+}g^{-}}\right)^{[2n+1]}
gives

\mathtt{Q}_{2|\emptyset}\propto-\frac{\mathtt{Q}_{12|1}}{\mathtt{Q}_{1|1}^{+}}+\mathtt{Q}_{1|\emptyset}\times\dots\,,

(139)

where the suppressed terms do not have cuts on the real axis. We see that the dressing factors in
\mathtt{Q}_{2|\emptyset}\mathtt{Q}_{\emptyset|1}
do not cancel out as happens for
\mathtt{Q}_{1|\emptyset}\mathtt{Q}_{\emptyset|1}
. This immediately implies
\overset{\scalebox{0.8}{\raisebox{-1.99306pt}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[2.0pt][l]{$\scriptstyle\hphantom{*}$}}\resizebox{0}{0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\approx$}}}}{\mathtt{Q}}\vphantom{\mathtt{Q}}^{-}_{2|1}\neq\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{2|1}

and so from (
127
) it follows that
(\bar{\mu}_{\text{as}})_{2\dot{1}}
does not have a square-root cut. Thus the no-square root property can be seen in the large volume limit, not only for some Q-functions but also for some
\bar{\mu}_{a\dot{a}}
.

Before closing this section we comment on the appearance of massless modes. Turning off all massive excitations, Bethe equations sourced by massless modes
z_{k}^{\pm}\equiv(z_{k})^{\pm 1}
are

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{M_{\theta}}\frac{y_{i}-z^{-}_{k}}{y_{i}-z^{+}_{k}}=1\,.

(140)

One can notice that these will be auxiliary equations,
\frac{\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{1|1}}{\mathcal{Q}_{1|1}^{-}}=1
, in the type-B QSC with
F=F_{\text{pol}}
, under the replacement
\theta_{k}\to z_{k}
, and assuming momentum conservation
\prod z_{k}=1
. This suggests that redefining
\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}\to\mathtt{f}_{\text{tot}}\prod\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}F_{\text{pol}}^{[2n]}
might be a way to include massless modes into the above analysis.

8
Conclusions and discussion

In this paper we proposed a method to derive quantum spectral curves of AdS/CFT type. The method consists of the special prescription for analytic continuation and the monodromy requirement: Q-system, a fused flag encoding conserved charges, transforms by a symmetry after the continuation. In the considered examples, and we believe that the statement is general, the resulting QSC’s are labelled by the equivalence classes
\mathsf{Out}\simeq\mathsf{Aut}/\mathsf{Inn}
, where
\mathsf{Inn}
is the group of continuous symmetries (H-rotations and gauge transformations) and
\mathsf{Aut}
are all symmetries (including also the discrete ones: Hodge duality and the swap of left and right Lie groups). For the
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
case, there are two options (cases A and B), and for the
\mathsf{SU}(2|2)\times\mathsf{SU}(2|2)
there are eight options out of which the two are interesting (cases C and D).

The focus of the proposed classification is to distinguish between the emerging Riemann-Hilbert problems. On top of them, extra analytic requirements such as asymptotic behaviour at infinity or additional periodicity features are needed to nail down the concrete physical system, and different systems are possible with the same type of monodromy behaviour. For instance, we explained in Section
6
that the type-B system with square root cuts can encode Lieb-Wu equations, their inhomogeneous generalisation, and also thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz equations for Hubbard model, depending on which additional analytic requirements do we choose. These options are unlikely to form the exhaustive set of possible physics described by the type-B QSC. For one more possibility, let us mention q-deformation of the inhomogeneous Hubbard model
Beisert:2008tw
. We expect that the same QSC should describe it, an explicit analysis verifying this proposal is yet to be done.

A situation when an automorphism of the system of conserved charges (Q-system or T-system) is emerging in the result of ‘large’ change of spectral parameter appears also in other integrable systems. For instance, in the case of integrable spin chains with boundary described as representations of twisted Yangians or quantum twisted affine algebras (or related systems,
e.g.
in the context of ODE/IM), the automorphism is due when one makes a reflection
u\to-u
(rational case) or the half-way rotation
z\to e^{\mathtt{i}\pi}z
(trigonometric case), see for instance
Molev:1994rs
;
Masoero:2015rcz
;
2016Guay
;
Frenkel:2016gxg
. These examples deal with entire functions of the spectral parameter so the stated large change of
u
or
z
is achieved by the unambiguous analytic continuation. Furthermore, since
u=0
and
z=0
are regular points in these examples, all possible automorphisms should become ‘inner’ (continuous) which forces folding of Dynkin diagram if the automorphism involves the reflection of this diagram
28
28

28

An alternative is to create two copies of Q-systems and fold them into one by the relation of style
Q_{\rm copy~1}(u)=Q_{\rm copy~2}^{*}(-u)
.
.

By contrast in our case, the large change of
u
is the encircling of the branch point
u={\mbox{h}}
, and a naive attempt of the analytic continuation interferes with the non-locality of Q-system. We circumvent this difficulty by performing symmetry transformations
while
doing the continuation. In the result, we get a very non-trivial representative in
\mathsf{Aut}
of the equivalence class in
\mathsf{Out}
. For instance, a representative of the class
{\rm[Hodge]}
defining the type-B system is Hodge transformation supplemented with gauge transformations, with physically relevant information packaged in function
F
, and with H-rotations encoded through matrices
\mu
and
\omega
. Note that the mentioned continuous symmetries are even not global ones but defined either in the mirror or in the physical kinematics. Typically, the representative of the equivalence class
does not
square to the identity operation, taking higher powers does not generically become the identity either. A similar effect is observed in Janik’s crossing equation
Janik:2006dc
, and essentially due to the same reason – non-locality in the cut structure of the scattering matrix. In fact, the monodromy bootstrap is a rather natural development of the crossing equation idea.

The non-triviality of the equivalence class representative, although it does not imply
per se
, nicely correlates with the observation that Q-functions almost never have branch points of square root type (
i.e.
of the second order). While it was possible in higher-dimensional examples of AdS/CFT integrability, here we prove the no-go theorems that show that Hubbard model is the only possible exception in cases A,B,C.

The equivalence class is nearly fully fixed, in terms of the discontinuity relations on
\mu
and
\omega
. This allows us to close the system of Riemann-Hilbert problems that constitute a concrete realisation of QSC to solve. Function
F
is only partially constrained by the monodromy requirements, it plays the role of a source term similar to the role of Drinfeld polynomial in more conventional integrable systems. It is very appealing to provide a more solid justification to this so far rather casual observation, for instance to relate
F
and the evaluation representations in
Beisert:2006fmy
.

From a physicist’s perspective, the developed approach offers a possibility of deriving QSC’s while circumventing tedious and not always available TBA computations. We demonstrated that this is clearly the case on the example of Hubbard model where a plethora of other methods are available allowing us to make comparisons; And we used the monodromy bootstrap to give the concrete conjecture for the spectral curve of AdS
3
/CFT
2
integrability with AdS
{}_{3}\times
S
{}^{3}\times
T
4
background supported by RR-flux. In the latter case, a possibility to derive QSC from the currently available rather incomplete TBA data
Bombardelli:2018jkj
;
Fontanella:2019ury

29
29

29

There are also some TBA results for the NS-NS case
Dei:2018jyj
which one cannot use for an immediate comparison.
is unlikely. Presence of the massless modes considerably complicates all steps of the TBA analysis, from formulation of the initial assumptions till the explicit computations of the spectrum. We believe that the quantum spectral curve derived from the monodromy bootstrap should capture correctly both massive and massless modes. In the present paper, we only made checks against the asymptotic Bethe Ansatz in the massive sector, although the gained experience with Hubbard model offered us a hint towards incorporating the massless modes, we mention it at the end of Section
7
. With no doubts, this QSC conjecture requires further considerable verification both in massless and massive sectors, its status is much less established compared to its higher-dimensional cousins.

Applying the same arguments as for AdS
3
/CFT
2
, we can conjecture that the type-B system at zero central charge (
i.e.
when
F^{2}=1
in which case it also coincides with the type-A system) describes spectrum of AdS
2
/CFT
1
integrability. We have performed a similar analysis to the one of Section
7
for this scenario and obtained asymptotic Bethe equations that are
not consistent
with either the Bethe equations proposed in
Sorokin:2011rr
or with the finite gap equations in the same paper that were used to conjecture the Bethe equations. The reason for the discrepancy is not entirely clear and we decided to not include the AdS
2
/CFT
1
computation in the current work postponing it for future publications.

From a mathematician’s perspective, our approach seems to offer a possibility to derive many different QSC’s of AdS/CFT type thus changing a perspective on AdS/CFT integrability: if previously the known examples were often viewed as isolated points, now these points start to fit into a landscape of novel-type integrable systems which is comparable in variability to well-established rational or trigonometric cases. We note that these AdS/CFT integrable systems are not of elliptic type either, we should rather expect that they are built on top of rational, trigonometric, and probably elliptic cases by introducing a possibility for branch points.

In future research, it would be important to explicitly verify whether the monodromy bootstrap indeed works for a variety of other groups to support the above claim. The equations presented in Section
5
do not depend on the group rank and hence we expect them to be valid for
\mathsf{SU}(N|N)
and
\mathsf{SU}(N|N)\times\mathsf{SU}(N|N)
systems. Whether we can also assume non-zero Coxeter number and consider
\mathsf{SU}(N|M)
systems remains unclear. Departing from A-series and considering orthosymplectic groups is even more intriguing because less is known there about the fully extended Q-system needed to properly describe the group of symmetry transformations. Two constructions are available in the literature that can be used for guidance in this research: QSC for AdS
4
/CFT
3
integrability
Bombardelli:2017vhk
, and the reproduction procedure on Bethe equations
Kang:2021bhx
which should be equivalent to applying Weyl reflections on Q-system, see
e.g.
explanations in
Ekhammar:2020enr
. Almost no information exists about extension of the
D(2,1;\alpha)
Q-system, doing this case has a clear physical motivation as it should describe AdS/CFT integrability with AdS
{}_{3}\times
S
{}^{3}\times
S
{}^{3}\times
T
1
background.

Of course, QSC provides only information about the spectrum. Probably the most fundamental question to ask what is the underlying quantum algebra
30
30

30

For AdS
5
/CFT
4
there are doubts whether it is even a Hopf algebra, if yes then with highly non-trivial braiding, hence we avoid using the term ‘quantum group’.
whose representation is the physical model and whose Bethe algebra, a maximal commutative subalgebra of conserved charges, is encoded by QSC. This quantum algebra would be an analog of Yangian or quantum affine algebra, or their twisted versions. The best handle we have to date is inhomogeneous Hubbard model where the looked-for algebra is presumably known: it is Yangian for centrally extended
{\mathsf{su}}(2|2)
. Its first Drinfeld and RTT realisations are known, and some basic examples of fusion were performed
Beisert:2006fmy
;
Beisert:2014hya
;
Beisert:2015msa

31
31

31

See also
Matsumoto:2008ww
;
Borsato:2017lpf
and references therein for derivations of the mentioned Yangian as contraction from
\mathfrak{d}(2,1;\alpha)
case.
. Yet, the universal R-matrix is unknown and neither systematic representation theory was developed. Also, Q-functions seem to be constructible as eigenvalues of Q-operators in ‘prefundamental’ representations
meneghelli_2020
, but more research should be done to confirm this point. Having at hand Hubbard QSC derived in this paper can be a valuable organising principle to guide future study of these questions.

Acknowledgements.

A major obstacle in concluding this work was unwillingness of the authors to accept the possibility of non-square root branch points.
We would like to thank Bogdan Stefański and Olof Ohlsson Sax for numerous conference talks and private discussions over the years that eventually persuaded us to overcome this bias.
